1
 
Exotic
 
Physics
 
Teleportation
 
of
 
Macroscopic
 
Matter:
 
A
 
Unified
 
Theoretical
 
Framework
 
Integrating
 
Traversable
 
Wormholes,
 
Negative
 
Energy
 
Engineering,
 
Quantum
 
Vacuum
 
Control,
 
Metric
 
Engineering,
 
Topological
 
Space-Time
 
Surgery,
 
Closed
 
Timelike
 
Curves,
 
Quantum
 
Foam
 
Shortcuts,
 
and
 
Energy-to-Geometry
 
Conversion
 
 
 
Mohammad
 
Belal
 
Ansari
 
Independent
 
Researcher
 
 
Email:
 
mbansarigkp99@gmail.com
 
 
 
Abstract
 
Macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
of
 
human-scale
 
matter,
 
when
 
formulated
 
within
 
general
 
relativity,
 
can
 
be
 
interpreted
 
as
 
transport
 
through
 
engineered
 
space–time
 
geometries
 
that
 
provide
 
effective
 
shortcuts
 
between
 
distant
 
regions
 
while
 
preserving
 
worldline
 
continuity.
 
In
 
this
 
work,
 
we
 
present
 
a
 
unified
 
theoretical
 
analysis
 
of
 
the
 
principal
 
exotic-geometry
 
candidates
 
for
 
such
 
transport,
 
emphasizing
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
and
 
warp/bubble
 
spacetimes
 
as
 
explicit
 
metric
 
constructions.
 
Using
 
the
 
Morris–Thorne
 
framework,
 
the
 
throat
 
flaring-out
 
condition
 
is
 
shown
 
to
 
imply
 
unavoidable
 
null
 
energy
 
condition
 
(NEC)
 
violation
 
in
 
the
 
vicinity
 
of
 
the
 
wormhole
 
throat.
 
We
 
then
 
discuss
 
negative
 
energy
 
as
 
a
 
necessary
 
enabling
 
resource
 
and
 
review
 
experimentally
 
grounded
 
vacuum
 
effects
 
such
 
as
 
the
 
Casimir
 
configuration
 
as
 
an
 
existence
 
proof
 
for
 
local
 
negative
 
energy
 
densities.
 
The
 
feasibility
 
of
 
macroscopic
 
shortcut
 
geometries
 
is
 
further
 
constrained
 
by
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
and
 
semiclassical
 
backreaction,
 
which
 
restrict
 
the
 
magnitude,
 
duration,
 
and
 
localization
 
of
 
negative
 
energy
 
required
 
for
 
stabilization.
 
Warp/bubble
 
metrics
 
are
 
analyzed
 
as
 
effective
 
transport
 
geometries
 
that
 
similarly
 
demand
 
exotic
 
stress–energy
 
in
 
the
 
boundary
 
layer
 
and
 
may
 
introduce
 
control
 
limitations
 
and
 
causality
 
risks.
 
Finally,
 
we
 
consolidate
 
these
 
results
 
into
 
a
 
single
 
constraint-based
 
perspective:
 
while
 
shortcut
 
geometries
 
are
 
mathematically
 
admissible,
 
physically
 
realizable
 
macroscopic
 
implementations
 
remain
 
strongly
 
limited
 
by
 
energy-condition
 
requirements,
 
quantum-field
 
constraints,
 
and
 
global
 
causality
 
considerations.
 
 
Keywords
:
 
Traversable
 
wormholes;
 
energy
 
conditions;
 
null
 
energy
 
condition
 
violation;
 
negative
 
energy;
 
Casimir
 
effect;
 
quantum
 
inequalities;
 
semiclassical
 
gravity;
 
warp
 
drive
 
spacetimes;
 
causality;
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
 
PACS
 
numbers
:
 
04.20.Gz,
 
04.20.Jb,
 
04.62.+v,
 
04.60.-m,
 
03.70.+k,
 
12.20.-m,
 
04.90.+e

from

First

deeply

of

1.
 
Introduction
 
Teleportation
 
is
 
commonly
 
described
 
as
 
the
 
instantaneous
 
transport
 
of
 
matter
 
from
 
one
 
point
 
in
 
space
 
to
 
another
 
without
 
continuous
 
motion
 
through
 
the
 
intervening
 
region.
 
In
 
popular
 
representations,
 
a
 
subject
 
disappears
 
at
 
a
 
departure
 
location
 
and
 
reappears
 
at
 
a
 
destination,
 
with
 
little
 
attention
 
paid
 
to
 
the
 
physical
 
mechanisms
 
needed
 
to
 
preserve
 
identity,
 
structure,
 
and
 
causality.
 
Scientific
 
inquiry,
 
by
 
contrast,
 
requires
 
teleportation
 
to
 
be
 
formulated
 
in
 
terms
 
of
 
well-defined
 
dynamical
 
laws
 
governing
 
matter,
 
fields,
 
and
 
space-time
 
geometry.
 
Any
 
claim
 
of
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
must
 
therefore
 
confront
 
three
 
foundational
 
issues:
 
(i)
 
the
 
causal
 
structure
 
of
 
space-time,
 
which
 
constrains
 
how
 
influence
 
and
 
information
 
propagate;
 
(ii)
 
the
 
energy–geometry
 
coupling
 
of
 
general
 
relativity,
 
which
 
determines
 
how
 
matter
 
shapes
 
the
 
metric;
 
and
 
(iii)
 
the
 
quantum
 
nature
 
of
 
matter
 
and
 
fields,
 
which
 
sets
 
limits
 
on
 
measurement,
 
state
 
reconstruction,
 
and
 
permissible
 
energy
 
distributions.
 
[1]
 
[2]
 
[6]
 
At
 
terrestrial
 
and
 
near-Earth
 
scales,
 
transport
 
is
 
limited
 
primarily
 
by
 
engineering:
 
propulsion,
 
materials,
 
infrastructure,
 
and
 
energy
 
efficiency.
 
At
 
interplanetary
 
scales,
 
travel
 
time
 
becomes
 
a
 
more
 
substantial
 
barrier,
 
and
 
at
 
interstellar
 
scales,
 
classical
 
travel
 
is
 
dominated
 
by
 
relativistic
 
constraints
 
and
 
enormous
 
distances.
 
Even
 
at
 
a
 
fraction
 
of
 
the
 
speed
 
of
 
light,
 
journeys
 
between
 
stars
 
require
 
years
 
or
 
centuries,
 
and
 
any
 
attempt
 
to
 
accelerate
 
massive
 
payloads
 
to
 
relativistic
 
speeds
 
implies
 
extraordinary
 
energy
 
costs.
 
These
 
constraints
 
have
 
motivated
 
the
 
study
 
of
 
non-classical
 
transport
 
ideas
 
in
 
theoretical
 
physics,
 
particularly
 
those
 
involving
 
space-time
 
geometries
 
that
 
may
 
provide
 
“shortcuts”
 
across
 
large
 
distances.
 
[2]
 
[3]
 
[7]
 
The
 
central
 
hypothesis
 
explored
 
in
 
this
 
thesis
 
is
 
that
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation—defined
 
here
 
as
 
the
 
relocation
 
of
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
or
 
large
 
matter
 
distribution
 
from
 
one
 
region
 
of
 
the
 
universe
 
to
 
another
 
without
 
conventional
 
traversal
 
of
 
intermediate
 
space—could,
 
in
 
principle,
 
be
 
realized
 
if
 
space-time
 
itself
 
can
 
be
 
engineered
 
into
 
a
 
configuration
 
that
 
provides
 
a
 
short
 
path
 
between
 
the
 
two
 
regions.
 
Within
 
general
 
relativity,
 
the
 
existence
 
of
 
such
 
paths
 
is
 
related
 
to
 
the
 
global
 
topology
 
and
 
local
 
curvature
 
of
 
the
 
metric.
 
In
 
certain
 
solutions
 
to
 
Einstein’s
 
field
 
equations,
 
geodesics
 
can
 
connect
 
distant
 
regions
 
with
 
shorter
 
proper
 
lengths
 
than
 
would
 
be
 
possible
 
in
 
ordinary
 
flat
 
space.
 
Wormholes
 
represent
 
the
 
best-known
 
example
 
of
 
this
 
concept.
 
[4]
 
[8]
 
However,
 
“in
 
principle”
 
does
 
not
 
mean
 
“physically
 
achievable.”
 
Many
 
exotic
 
space-time
 
geometries,
 
including
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
and
 
warp-bubble
 
metrics,
 
require
 
stress–energy
 
tensors
 
that
 
violate
 
classical
 
energy
 
conditions.
 
The
 
energy
 
conditions—such
 
as
 
the
 
Null
 
Energy
 
Condition
 
(NEC)—capture
 
physically
 
intuitive
 
restrictions
 
on
 
the
 
positivity
 
of
 
energy
 
density
 
measured
 
by
 
observers
 
and
 
the
 
focusing
 
behavior
 
of
 
null
 
geodesic
 
congruences.
 
Traversable
 
wormholes,
 
in
 
particular,
 
generally
 
require
 
NEC
 
violation
 
near
 
the
 
throat
 
in
 
order
 
to
 
satisfy
 
the
 
geometric
 
“flaring-out”
 
condition
 
that
 
prevents
 
the
 
wormhole
 
from
 
pinching
 
off.
 
Classical
 
matter
 
fields
 
typically
 
satisfy
 
the
 
NEC,
 
and
 
thus
 
cannot
 
provide
 
the
 
required
 
stress–energy
 
configuration.
 
[4]
 
[9]
 
Quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
modifies
 
this
 
conclusion
 
by
 
allowing
 
localized
 
negative
 
energy
 
densities
 
under
 
specific
 
conditions,
 
such
 
as
 
in
 
squeezed
 
states
 
or
 
in
 
the
 
Casimir
 
effect.
 
Yet
 
quantum
 
theory
 
also
 
imposes
 
strong
 
constraints
 
on
 
how
 
negative
 
energy
 
can
 
be
 
distributed
 
in
 
time
 
and
 
space.
 
In
 
semiclassical
 
gravity,
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
restrict
 
the
 
magnitude
 
and
 
duration
 
of
 
negative
 
energy
 
observed
 
along
 
timelike
 
worldlines,
 
severely
 
limiting
 
the
 
feasibility
 
of
 
maintaining
 
macroscopic
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
using
 
realistic
 
quantum
 
sources.
 
These
 
constraints
 
do
 
not
 
mathematically
 
forbid
 
wormholes,
 
but
 
they
 
shift
 
the
 
challenge
 
from
 
geometry
 
to
 
engineering:
 
the
 
problem
 
becomes
 
whether
 
one
 
can
 
produce
 
and
 
stabilize
 
an
 
exotic
 
stress–energy
 
distribution
 
at
 
scales
 
that
 
are
 
compatible
 
with
 
human-safe
 
traversal.
 
[9]
 
[10]
 
The
 
aim
 
of
 
this
 
thesis
 
is
 
not
 
to
 
present
 
a
 
claim
 
of
 
an
 
already-buildable
 
teleportation
 
device.
 
Instead,
 
the
 
aim
 
is
 
to
 
provide
 
a
 
rigorous
 
theoretical
 
integration
 
of
 
eight
 
exotic
 
transport
 
concepts
 
and
 
to

===== PAGE 5 =====
5
 
analyze
 
which
 
of
 
them
 
is
 
most
 
plausible
 
for
 
eventual
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation,
 
assuming
 
extremely
 
advanced
 
capabilities.
 
The
 
eight
 
concepts
 
are:
 
 
1.
 
Traversable
 
Wormholes
,
 
primarily
 
in
 
the
 
Morris–Thorne
 
framework,
 
which
 
provide
 
explicit
 
metrics
 
for
 
throat-stabilized
 
shortcuts
 
between
 
distant
 
regions.
 
[4]
 
2.
 
Negative
 
Energy
 
Engineering
 
(Casimir-based),
 
which
 
examines
 
the
 
only
 
experimentally
 
verified
 
mechanism
 
producing
 
negative
 
energy
 
density
 
and
 
evaluates
 
scaling
 
limits.
 
[11]
 
3.
 
Quantum
 
Vacuum
 
State
 
Manipulation,
 
which
 
explores
 
whether
 
vacuum
 
phase
 
structure
 
and
 
field
 
configurations
 
can
 
be
 
controlled
 
to
 
achieve
 
macroscopic
 
transport.
 
[12]
 
4.
 
Space-Time
 
Metric
 
Engineering
 
(Warp/Bubble),
 
focusing
 
on
 
geometries
 
that
 
produce
 
effective
 
superluminal
 
motion
 
relative
 
to
 
distant
 
observers
 
by
 
contracting
 
and
 
expanding
 
space.
 
[13]
 
5.
 
Topological
 
Space-Time
 
Surgery,
 
which
 
studies
 
the
 
mathematical
 
possibility
 
of
 
topology
 
change
 
or
 
nontrivial
 
connectivity
 
that
 
could
 
enable
 
distant
 
identification.
 
[14]
 
6.
 
Closed
 
Timelike
 
Curve
 
(CTC)
 
Assisted
 
Jump,
 
which
 
examines
 
transport
 
mechanisms
 
associated
 
with
 
causal
 
loops
 
and
 
their
 
paradoxes.
 
[15]
 
7.
 
Quantum
 
Foam
 
Shortcuts
 
(Planck-scale),
 
which
 
investigates
 
the
 
possibility
 
that
 
microscopic
 
wormholes
 
or
 
nontrivial
 
topology
 
exists
 
at
 
Planck
 
lengths
 
and
 
might
 
be
 
exploited.
 
[16]
 
8.
 
Energy-to-Geometry
 
Conversion,
 
which
 
frames
 
teleportation
 
as
 
the
 
ultimate
 
problem
 
of
 
controlling
 
stress–energy
 
distributions
 
to
 
generate
 
desired
 
geometry.
 
[1]
 
[2]
 
 
A
 
key
 
methodological
 
choice
 
is
 
to
 
treat
 
these
 
concepts
 
as
 
part
 
of
 
a
 
single
 
conceptual
 
family
 
rather
 
than
 
as
 
unrelated
 
proposals.
 
The
 
family
 
resemblance
 
arises
 
from
 
Einstein’s
 
field
 
equations:
 
space-time
 
geometry
 
is
 
not
 
a
 
passive
 
background
 
but
 
a
 
dynamical
 
object
 
determined
 
by
 
energy–momentum.
 
Therefore,
 
any
 
sufficiently
 
powerful
 
technology
 
capable
 
of
 
producing
 
and
 
shaping
 
stress–energy
 
distributions
 
could,
 
in
 
principle,
 
shape
 
space-time
 
itself.
 
Teleportation
 
in
 
the
 
exotic-physics
 
sense
 
thus
 
becomes
 
a
 
question
 
of
 
whether
 
one
 
can
 
intentionally
 
sculpt
 
a
 
metric
 
that
 
connects
 
distant
 
regions
 
and
 
provides
 
stable,
 
traversable
 
worldlines
 
for
 
macroscopic
 
matter.
 
[1]
 
[2]
 
The
 
thesis
 
also
 
emphasizes
 
the
 
distinction
 
between
 
information
 
teleportation
 
and
 
matter
 
teleportation.
 
Quantum
 
teleportation,
 
experimentally
 
demonstrated
 
for
 
quantum
 
states,
 
transfers
 
quantum
 
information
 
between
 
systems
 
using
 
entanglement
 
and
 
classical
 
communication.
 
It
 
does
 
not
 
transport
 
macroscopic
 
matter
 
through
 
space,
 
and
 
it
 
does
 
not
 
bypass
 
the
 
light-speed
 
constraint
 
for
 
classical
 
information
 
transfer.
 
Although
 
quantum
 
teleportation
 
is
 
a
 
profound
 
scientific
 
achievement,
 
it
 
addresses
 
a
 
different
 
problem
 
than
 
the
 
relocation
 
of
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
as
 
matter.
 
For
 
this
 
reason,
 
quantum
 
teleportation
 
is
 
not
 
considered
 
the
 
primary
 
mechanism
 
of
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
in
 
this
 
thesis;
 
instead,
 
it
 
provides
 
conceptual
 
support
 
for
 
the
 
notion
 
that
 
“state
 
transfer”
 
can
 
occur
 
without
 
moving
 
the
 
carrier
 
particle
 
in
 
a
 
classical
 
sense.
 
[17]
 
[18]
 
To
 
meaningfully
 
discuss
 
the
 
teleportation
 
of
 
a
 
human
 
body,
 
one
 
must
 
also
 
introduce
 
biophysical
 
survivability
 
constraints.
 
In
 
any
 
extreme
 
space-time
 
geometry,
 
a
 
traveler
 
may
 
be
 
exposed
 
to
 
tidal
 
acceleration
 
gradients
 
that
 
tear
 
matter
 
apart,
 
to
 
high-energy
 
radiation,
 
or
 
to
 
horizon-like
 
surfaces
 
where
 
field
 
effects
 
become
 
catastrophic.
 
A
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
mechanism
 
must
 
therefore
 
satisfy
 
not
 
only
 
mathematical
 
existence
 
but
 
also
 
physical
 
conditions
 
ensuring
 
that
 
the
 
integrated
 
stress,
 
acceleration,
 
and
 
energy
 
deposition
 
experienced
 
by
 
the
 
traveler
 
remains
 
within
 
survivable
 
bounds.
 
Wormholes
 
are
 
attractive
 
in
 
this
 
context
 
because
 
they
 
provide
 
a
 
continuous
 
path
 
through
 
space-time;
 
a
 
traveler
 
need
 
not
 
be
 
disassembled
 
and
 
reconstructed,
 
and
 
the
 
subject’s
 
worldline
 
can
 
remain
 
continuous.
 
This
 
continuity
 
is
 
closely
 
related
 
to
 
preserving
 
physical
 
identity
 
in
 
a
 
physically
 
meaningful
 
sense.
 
[4]
 
[8]

===== PAGE 6 =====
6
 
The
 
remainder
 
of
 
the
 
thesis
 
proceeds
 
by
 
establishing
 
the
 
theoretical
 
foundations
 
required
 
to
 
evaluate
 
exotic
 
transport
 
proposals.
 
This
 
includes
 
the
 
Einstein
 
field
 
equations,
 
the
 
role
 
of
 
the
 
stress–energy
 
tensor,
 
energy
 
conditions
 
and
 
their
 
physical
 
interpretation,
 
and
 
semiclassical
 
constraints
 
from
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
in
 
curved
 
space-time.
 
After
 
building
 
this
 
foundation,
 
each
 
of
 
the
 
eight
 
concepts
 
is
 
analyzed
 
through
 
a
 
consistent
 
framework:
 
definition
 
of
 
the
 
proposed
 
mechanism,
 
mathematical
 
expression
 
of
 
the
 
geometry
 
or
 
field
 
configuration,
 
required
 
energy–momentum
 
content,
 
stability
 
and
 
causality
 
considerations,
 
scaling
 
obstacles
 
for
 
human
 
and
 
large-matter
 
teleportation,
 
and
 
the
 
most
 
plausible
 
research
 
pathway
 
for
 
progress.
 
Finally,
 
the
 
thesis
 
provides
 
a
 
unified
 
feasibility
 
ranking
 
and
 
a
 
structured
 
roadmap
 
for
 
future
 
research
 
priorities,
 
with
 
particular
 
focus
 
on
 
negative
 
energy
 
engineering
 
and
 
metric
 
stabilization.
 
In
 
doing
 
so,
 
the
 
thesis
 
aims
 
to
 
achieve
 
two
 
outcomes:
 
first,
 
to
 
clearly
 
state
 
what
 
is
 
mathematically
 
required
 
to
 
teleport
 
macroscopic
 
matter
 
anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe
 
within
 
the
 
context
 
of
 
relativity
 
and
 
quantum
 
theory;
 
second,
 
to
 
identify
 
which
 
theoretical
 
obstacles
 
are
 
most
 
fundamental
 
and
 
which
 
might
 
be
 
gradually
 
reduced
 
through
 
future
 
advances
 
in
 
quantum
 
vacuum
 
control,
 
high-energy
 
field
 
manipulation,
 
and
 
quantum
 
gravity.
 
[3]
 
[9]
 
[10]
 
 
2.
 
Contributions
 
and
 
Scope
 
of
 
This
 
Work
 
This
 
work
 
investigates
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
within
 
the
 
established
 
language
 
of
 
gravitational
 
and
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory.
 
Here,
 
teleportation
 
is
 
used
 
in
 
the
 
geometric
 
sense:
 
the
 
existence
 
of
 
a
 
continuous,
 
physically
 
admissible
 
worldline
 
connecting
 
a
 
departure
 
region
 
to
 
a
 
distant
 
arrival
 
region
 
through
 
a
 
nontrivial
 
space–time
 
geometry,
 
rather
 
than
 
through
 
disassembly
 
and
 
reconstruction
 
of
 
matter.
 
The
 
analysis
 
is
 
therefore
 
focused
 
on
 
the
 
conditions
 
under
 
which
 
general
 
relativity
 
admits
 
shortcut
 
geometries
 
(such
 
as
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
and
 
warp/bubble
 
spacetimes),
 
and
 
on
 
the
 
stress–energy
 
requirements
 
needed
 
to
 
form
 
and
 
stabilize
 
them
 
within
 
semiclassical
 
constraints.
 
 
2.1
 
Primary
 
Contributions
 
 
1.
 
Unified
 
geometric
 
formulation
 
of
 
exotic
 
macroscopic
 
transport:
 
The
 
paper
 
organizes
 
multiple
 
exotic-transport
 
proposals
 
into
 
a
 
single
 
framework
 
based
 
on
 
the
 
Einstein
 
field

between
 
boundary
 
conditions,
 
vacuum
 
structure,
 
and
 
negative
 
energy.
 
If
 
future
 
technology
 
could
 
amplify
 
such
 
effects,
 
engineer
 
more
 
exotic
 
boundary
 
configurations,
 
or
 
exploit
 
analogous
 
mechanisms
 
in
 
other
 
quantum
 
fields,
 
Casimir-type
 
approaches
 
may
 
serve
 
as
 
a
 
stepping
 
stone
 
toward
 
controlled
 
negative
 
energy
 
distributions.
 
[40]
 
[41]
 
 
4.8
 
Quantum
 
Inequalities:
 
Why
 
Negative
 
Energy
 
Cannot
 
Be
 
Arbitrarily
 
Large
 
Even
 
though
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
allows
 
negative
 
energy
 
densities,
 
it
 
also
 
imposes
 
strict
 
restrictions.
 
The
 
primary
 
constraints
 
are
 
known
 
as
 
quantum
 
inequalities,
 
developed
 
to
 
quantify
 
the
 
fact
 
that
 
negative
 
energy
 
cannot
 
be
 
maintained
 
for
 
arbitrarily
 
long
 
durations
 
or
 
accumulated
 
without
 
compensation.
 
A
 
representative
 
form
 
of
 
these
 
constraints,
 
associated
 
with
 
Ford
 
and
 
Roman,
 
limits
 
the
 
averaged
 
energy
 
density
 
measured
 
by
 
an
 
observer
 
along
 
a
 
timelike
 
worldline.
 
In
 
simplified
 
form,
 
for
 
a
 
suitable
 
sampling
 
function
 
f(
τ)
 
of
 
characteristic
 
width
 
τ
0
 
,
 
one
 
has
 
an
 
inequality
 
of
 
the
 
schematic
 
type
 
 
 
 
where
 
u
μ
is
 
the
 
observer’s
 
four-velocity
 
and
 
K
 
is
 
a
 
constant
 
depending
 
on
 
the
 
field
 
theory
 
and
 
the
 
sampling
 
function.
 
While
 
the
 
detailed
 
form
 
varies
 
with
 
the
 
choice
 
of
 
quantum
 
field
 
and
 
spacetime
 
dimension,
 
the
 
key
 
implication
 
is
 
robust:
 
large
 
negative
 
energy
 
can
 
exist
 
only
 
for
 
very
 
short
 
times,
 
and
 
sustained
 
negative
 
energy
 
must
 
be
 
extremely
 
small.
 
[9]
 
[42]
 
This
 
principle
 
is
 
sometimes
 
described
 
informally
 
as
 
a
 
“quantum
 
interest”
 
effect:
 
if
 
negative
 
energy
 
appears,
 
it
 
must
 
be
 
compensated
 
by
 
positive
 
energy
 
nearby
 
in
 
time,
 
often
 
with
 
a
 
larger
 
magnitude.
 
As
 
a
 
consequence,
 
attempts
 
to
 
construct
 
macroscopic
 
wormholes
 
or
 
warp
 
bubbles
 
using
 
negative
 
energy
 
encounter
 
severe
 
obstacles.
 
The
 
geometry
 
demands
 
a
 
continuous
 
or
 
quasi-static
 
distribution
 
of
 
NEC-violating
 
stress–energy,
 
but
 
the
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
tend
 
to
 
prevent
 
exactly
 
such
 
distributions
 
from
 
being
 
stable
 
and
 
long-lasting
 
in
 
physically
 
realistic
 
quantum
 
states.
 
[42]
 
[43]
 
For
 
exotic
 
teleportation,
 
this
 
is
 
a
 
decisive
 
barrier:
 
the
 
required
 
negative
 
energy
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
technologically
 
hard
 
to
 
produce,
 
it
 
is
 
constrained
 
by
 
quantum
 
theory
 
itself.
 
If
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
are
 
fundamental
 
and
 
unavoidable,
 
then
 
any
 
practical
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
mechanism
 
must
 
either
 
find
 
a
 
way
 
to
 
work
 
within
 
these
 
bounds,
 
exploit
 
loopholes
 
such
 
as
 
specific
 
curved
 
backgrounds,
 
or
 
rely
 
on
 
new
 
physics
 
beyond
 
standard
 
semiclassical
 
gravity.
 
[22]
 
[44]
 
 
4.9
 
Backreaction:
 
Geometry
 
Responds
 
to
 
Quantum
 
Fields
 
Another
 
essential
 
issue
 
is
 
backreaction,
 
the
 
effect
 
of
 
quantum
 
fields
 
on
 
the
 
space-time
 
geometry
 
they
 
inhabit.
 
In
 
semiclassical
 
gravity,
 
negative
 
energy
 
configurations
 
are
 
not
 
placed
 
into
 
a
 
fixed
 
geometry
 
without
 
consequence;
 
rather,
 
they
 
feed
 
into
 
the
 
field
 
equations
 
and
 
alter
 
the
 
metric.
 
This
 
can
 
produce
 
instabilities:
 
a
 
negative
 
energy
 
region
 
intended
 
to
 
stabilize
 
a
 
wormhole
 
throat
 
may
 
instead
 
trigger
 
curvature
 
changes
 
that
 
collapse
 
the
 
throat,
 
generate
 
horizons,
 
or
 
create
 
singular
 
behavior.
 
In
 
general,

===== PAGE 14 =====
14
 
the
 
semiclassical
 
Einstein
 
equations
 
are
 
notoriously
 
difficult
 
to
 
solve
 
self-consistently
 
because
 
⟨
T̂
μ
v
⟩
 
depends
 
on
 
the
 
geometry,
 
and
 
the
 
geometry
 
depends
 
on
 
⟨
T̂
μ
v
⟩
.
 
[32]
 
[33]
 
Backreaction
 
becomes
 
especially
 
important
 
near
 
horizons
 
or
 
in
 
regions
 
of
 
rapidly
 
varying
 
curvature,
 
where
 
particle
 
creation
 
effects
 
can
 
become
 
large.
 
In
 
warp
 
and
 
wormhole
 
geometries,
 
horizon-like
 
surfaces
 
may
 
appear
 
for
 
certain
 
observers,
 
potentially
 
leading
 
to
 
strong
 
quantum
 
field
 
excitations.
 
These
 
excitations
 
can
 
deposit
 
energy
 
into
 
the
 
system,
 
potentially
 
destabilizing
 
the
 
very
 
structure
 
intended
 
to
 
provide
 
teleportation.
 
Thus,
 
even
 
if
 
a
 
negative
 
energy
 
distribution
 
could
 
be
 
engineered,
 
keeping
 
the
 
overall
 
geometry
 
stable
 
requires
 
a
 
deeper
 
understanding
 
of
 
semiclassical
 
dynamics.
 
[45]
 
[46]
 
 
4.10
 
Summary
 
of
 
Foundations:
 
The
 
Central
 
Bottleneck
 
The
 
foundational
 
analysis
 
leads
 
to
 
a
 
clear
 
conclusion:
 
exotic
 
teleportation
 
proposals
 
are
 
primarily
 
constrained
 
by
 
the
 
stress–energy
 
requirements
 
of
 
the
 
desired
 
geometry.
 
Traversable
 
wormholes,
 
warp
 
bubbles,
 
and
 
related
 
mechanisms
 
typically
 
require
 
violations
 
of
 
the
 
NEC,
 
which
 
implies
 
negative
 
energy
 
densities
 
or
 
exotic
 
pressures.
 
Quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
permits
 
negative
 
energy,
 
but
 
only
 
within
 
strict
 
limitations
 
imposed
 
by
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
and
 
the
 
need
 
for
 
compensating
 
positive
 
energy.
 
Additionally,
 
semiclassical
 
backreaction
 
and
 
stability
 
issues
 
strongly
 
suggest
 
that
 
maintaining
 
a
 
macroscopic,
 
human-traversable
 
exotic
 
geometry
 
would
 
require
 
unprecedented
 
control
 
over
 
quantum
 
states,
 
boundary
 
conditions,
 
and
 
gravitational
 
dynamics.
 
[9]
 
[22]
 
[44]
 
In
 
the
 
chapters
 
that
 
follow,
 
these
 
foundational
 
constraints
 
will
 
be
 
applied
 
consistently
 
across
 
the
 
eight
 
exotic
 
teleportation
 
concepts.
 
The
 
analysis
 
will
 
emphasize
 
that
 
the
 
most
 
promising
 
routes
 
are
 
not
 
those
 
that
 
merely
 
appear
 
mathematically
 
elegant,
 
but
 
those
 
that
 
possess
 
at
 
least
 
one
 
of
 
the
 
following:
 
(i)
 
an
 
experimental
 
foothold
 
in
 
known
 
physics,
 
(ii)
 
a
 
theoretically
 
consistent
 
stress–energy
 
source,
 
and
 
(iii)
 
a
 
plausible
 
stabilization
 
pathway.
 
Under
 
this
 
framework,
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
supported
 
by
 
engineered
 
negative
 
energy
 
remain
 
the
 
primary
 
candidate,
 
while
 
warp/bubble
 
metrics
 
and
 
quantum
 
vacuum
 
manipulation
 
serve
 
as
 
complementary
 
approaches
 
that
 
may
 
share
 
underlying
 
physics.
 
[21]
 
[13]
 
[22]
 
 
5.
 
Traversable
 
Wormholes
 
as
 
a
 
Macroscopic
 
Teleportation
 
Mechanism
 
Among
 
all
 
exotic
 
teleportation
 
proposals,
 
traversable
 
wormholes
 
occupy
 
a
 
unique
 
position
 
because
 
they
 
provide
 
an
 
explicit
 
and
 
mathematically
 
well-defined
 
mechanism
 
for
 
connecting
 
distant
 
regions
 
of
 
space-time
 
through
 
a
 
finite,
 
continuous,
 
and
 
potentially
 
human-traversable
 
path.
 
In
 
contrast
 
to
 
many
 
teleportation
 
ideas
 
that
 
rely
 
on
 
disassembly
 
and
 
reconstruction,
 
a
 
traversable
 
wormhole
 
offers
 
a
 
continuous
 
timelike
 
worldline
 
for
 
a
 
traveler:
 
the
 
person
 
enters
 
the
 
wormhole
 
mouth,
 
passes
 
through
 
the
 
throat,
 
and
 
exits
 
at
 
another
 
mouth
 
located
 
far
 
away
 
in
 
the
 
external
 
universe.
 
The
 
primary
 
promise
 
of
 
the
 
wormhole
 
paradigm
 
is
 
therefore
 
not
 
“instantaneous
 
transport”
 
in
 
a
 
strict
 
operational
 
sense,
 
but
 
rather
 
the
 
ability
 
to
 
reduce
 
effective
 
travel
 
distance
 
and
 
proper
 
time
 
by
 
engineering
 
space-time
 
connectivity.
 
The
 
main
 
obstacle
 
is
 
equally
 
clear:
 
the
 
wormhole
 
geometry
 
requires
 
matter
 
distributions
 
that
 
violate
 
classical
 
energy
 
conditions,
 
especially
 
the
 
Null
 
Energy
 
Condition
 
(NEC),
 
and
 
must
 
remain
 
stable
 
against
 
both
 
classical
 
and
 
quantum
 
perturbations.
 
[4]
 
[5]
 
[21]
 
[22]
 
[27]
 
 
5.1
 
Conceptual
 
Geometry:
 
Two
 
Asymptotic
 
Regions
 
Connected
 
by
 
a
 
Throat
 
A
 
traversable
 
wormhole
 
can
 
be

as

two

flat

by
 
a
 
“handle”
 
or
 
throat.
 
Far

resembles
 
ordinary
 
space-time,
 
but
 
near
 
the
 
throat
 
the
 
metric
 
deviates
 
strongly
 
from
 
flatness
 
to
 
maintain
 
an
 
open
 
passage.
 
A
 
key
 
feature
 
of
 
the
 
Morris–Thorne
 
construction
 
is
 
that
 
it
 
avoids
 
event
 
horizons
 
and
 
singularities,
 
ensuring
 
that
 
travelers
 
can
 
traverse
 
the
 
wormhole
 
in
 
finite
 
proper
 
time
 
and
 
that
 
signals
 
can
 
pass
 
in

===== PAGE 15 =====
15
 
both
 
directions.
 
This
 
is
 
essential
 
for
 
teleportation-like
 
transport,
 
since
 
an
 
event
 
horizon
 
would
 
prevent
 
return
 
signals
 
and
 
would
 
generally
 
imply
 
destructive
 
gravitational
 
effects.
 
[4]
 
[21]

5.8

“Anywhere

A

of

In

placed
 
arbitrarily
 
far
 
apart

external

2.

across
 
space,

transported
 
(classically
 
or

desired

and
 
then
 
used

an

===== PAGE 21 =====
21
 
In
 
the

A

and

[21]

5.9
 
Stability:
 
The
 
Hardest
 
Wormhole
 
Problem
 
Even

matter

against
 
perturbations.
 
Instabilities

5.13
 
Wormholes
 
as
 
a
 
Network
 
for
 
Universe-Wide
 
Teleportation
 
To
 
enable

“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe,”
 
one
 
must
 
go
 
beyond
 
a
 
single
 
wormhole
 
and
 
consider
 
a
 
wormhole
 
network.
 
Conceptually,
 
if
 
a
 
civilization
 
could
 
create
 
and
 
stabilize
 
many
 
wormholes
 
with
 
mouths
 
distributed
 
across
 
space,
 
teleportation
 
could
 
be
 
achieved
 
by
 
entering
 
a
 
mouth
 
at
 
the
 
origin
 
and
 
exiting
 
at
 
the
 
node
 
closest
 
to
 
the
 
destination.
 
The
 
idea
 
resembles
 
a
 
transportation
 
network
 
in
 
ordinary
 
engineering,
 
except
 
that
 
the
 
“links”
 
are
 
geometric
 
shortcuts
 
rather
 
than
 
tunnels
 
through
 
space.
 
[22]
 
[52]
 
From
 
a
 
graph
 
perspective,
 
let
 
nodes
 
represent
 
wormhole
 
mouths
 
and
 
edges
 
represent
 
wormhole
 
throats.
 
The
 
travel
 
time
 
between
 
nodes
 
would
 
then
 
be
 
dominated
 
by
 
traversal
 
time
 
through
 
the
 
throat,
 
not
 
by
 
external
 
distance.
 
However,
 
the
 
existence
 
of
 
such
 
a
 
network
 
raises
 
deep
 
questions:
 
1.
 
Placement
 
problem:
 
how
 
are
 
mouths
 
positioned
 
across
 
cosmic
 
distances?
 
2.
 
Stabilization
 
problem:
 
how
 
is
 
exotic
 
matter
 
supplied
 
to
 
each
 
throat?
 
3.
 
Causality
 
problem:
 
can
 
network
 
loops
 
generate
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves?
 
4.
 
Security/containment
:
 
how
 
does
 
one
 
prevent
 
catastrophic
 
instabilities
 
or
 
misuse?
 
The
 
causality
 
issue
 
is
 
particularly
 
severe:
 
networks
 
of
 
wormholes
 
may
 
allow
 
time-machine-like
 
structures
 
even
 
if
 
individual
 
wormholes
 
are
 
initially
 
“safe.”
 
Chronology
 
protection
 
arguments
 
suggest
 
that
 
quantum
 
effects
 
may
 
act
 
to
 
prevent
 
macroscopic
 
CTC
 
formation,
 
potentially
 
destabilizing
 
wormholes
 
if
 
a
 
time-machine
 
configuration
 
is
 
approached.
 
Therefore,
 
a
 
universe-wide
 
teleportation
 
network
 
may
 
be
 
fundamentally
 
limited
 
by
 
causality
 
enforcement
 
mechanisms,
 
if
 
such
 
mechanisms
 
exist.

[22]

7.
 
Space-Time
 
Metric
 
Engineering
 
(Warp/Bubble)
 
as
 
Effective
 
Teleportation
 
Traversable
 
wormholes
 
represent
 
a
 
topological
 
shortcut:
 
they
 
connect
 
two
 
distant
 
regions
 
through
 
a
 
throat
 
and
 
allow
 
matter
 
to
 
traverse
 
that
 
link.
 
A
 
conceptually
 
distinct
 
approach
 
is
 
space-time
 
metric
 
engineering,
 
in
 
which
 
space-time
 
itself
 
is
 
dynamically
 
shaped
 
such
 
that
 
a
 
localized
 
region
 
of
 
space—often
 
described
 
as
 
a
 
“bubble”—moves
 
relative
 
to
 
distant
 
observers
 
in
 
a
 
way
 
that
 
produces
 
effective
 
faster-than-light
 
(FTL)
 
transport
 
without
 
locally
 
exceeding
 
the
 
speed
 
of
 
light.
 
In
 
popular
 
terms,
 
this
 
is
 
sometimes
 
labeled
 
a
 
“warp
 
drive.”
 
While
 
warp
 
geometries
 
are
 
not
 
identical
 
to
 
teleportation,
 
they
 
share
 
the
 
same
 
objective:
 
to
 
enable
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
and
 
large
 
matter
 
to
 
reach
 
extremely
 
distant
 
locations
 
in
 
arbitrarily
 
small
 
external
 
time.
 
In
 
this
 
thesis,
 
warp/bubble
 
metrics
 
are
 
therefore
 
treated
 
as
 
a
 
teleportation-adjacent
 
mechanism,
 
especially
 
relevant
 
for
 
the
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe”
 
requirement,
 
because
 
they
 
do
 
not
 
require
 
a
 
pre-positioned
 
wormhole
 
mouth
 
at
 
the
 
destination.
 
[13]
 
[22]
 
[54]
 
[56]
 
 
7.1
 
Core
 
Idea:
 
Move
 
Space-Time,
 
Not
 
the
 
Traveler
 
In
 
special
 
relativity,
 
no
 
material
 
object
 
can
 
locally
 
move
 
faster
 
than
 
light.
 
In
 
general
 
relativity,
 
however,
 
the
 
expansion
 
and
 
contraction
 
of
 
space
 
itself
 
is
 
not
 
constrained
 
in
 
the
 
same
 
way,
 
as
 
demonstrated
 
by
 
cosmological
 
expansion.
 
Warp
 
metrics
 
exploit
 
this
 
by
 
proposing
 
a
 
geometry
 
in
 
which
 
a
 
compact
 
region
 
of
 
space-time—containing
 
the
 
traveler
 
and
 
payload—remains
 
locally
 
near-flat
 
and
 
timelike,
 
while
 
the
 
surrounding
 
metric
 
is
 
distorted
 
such
 
that
 
the
 
region
 
is
 
effectively
 
transported
 
rapidly
 
across
 
external
 
coordinate
 
distances.
 
The
 
traveler
 
is
 
not
 
“accelerated”
 
through
 
space
 
in
 
the
 
usual
 
sense;
 
rather,
 
the
 
metric
 
evolves
 
so
 
that
 
the
 
bubble’s
 
center
 
follows
 
a
 
worldline
 
that
 
can
 
connect
 
distant
 
points
 
in
 
a
 
short
 
external
 
coordinate
 
time.
 
[13]
 
[6]
 
[24]
 
This
 
concept
 
can
 
be
 
stated
 
geometrically:
 
if
 
the
 
metric
 
is
 
engineered
 
so
 
that
 
geodesics
 
within
 
the
 
bubble
 
remain
 
comfortable
 
while
 
the
 
bubble’s
 
boundary
 
induces
 
expansion
 
behind
 
and
 
contraction
 
ahead,
 
the
 
bubble
 
can
 
translate
 
relative
 
to
 
distant
 
observers.
 
This
 
parallels
 
the
 
intuition
 
of
 
“surfing”
 
on
 
a
 
space-time
 
wave.
 
However,
 
converting
 
this
 
intuition
 
into
 
a
 
viable
 
physical
 
mechanism
 
requires
 
specifying
 
an
 
explicit
 
metric
 
and
 
computing
 
its
 
required
 
stress–energy
 
tensor.
 
[13]
 
[54]

[22]

with

of

|
∇
f|
 
~
 
1/δ

7.5
 
Horizon
 
Structure
 
and
 
Control
 
Problem
 
Warp
 
geometries
 
introduce
 
an
 
additional
 
issue:
 
for
 
superluminal
 
bubble
 
velocities
 
v
s
 
>
 
c,
 
the
 
spacetime
 
develops
 
horizon-like
 
features.
 
In
 
effect,
 
signals
 
from
 
inside
 
the
 
bubble
 
may
 
not
 
be
 
able
 
to
 
reach
 
certain
 
regions
 
outside,
 
and
 
external
 
signals
 
may
 
not
 
be
 
able
 
to
 
reach
 
the
 
interior,
 
depending
 
on
 
direction.
 
This
 
creates
 
a
 
severe
 
operational
 
limitation:
 
the
 
traveler
 
might
 
not
 
be
 
able
 
to
 
control
 
or
 
stop
 
the
 
bubble
 
once
 
it
 
is
 
moving
 
superluminally,
 
because
 
signals
 
cannot
 
propagate
 
from
 
the
 
interior
 
to
 
the
 
bubble
 
front
 
boundary
 
that
 
determines
 
motion.
 
This
 
“control
 
problem”
 
undermines
 
the
 
notion
 
of
 
a
 
practical
 
teleportation
 
device,
 
since
 
a
 
technology
 
that
 
cannot
 
be
 
controlled
 
or
 
navigated
 
safely
 
is
 
not
 
functional
 
for
 
human
 
transport.
 
[49]
 
[54]
 
Additionally,
 
horizon
 
formation
 
is
 
closely
 
linked
 
to
 
quantum
 
field
 
instabilities.
 
Horizon-like
 
surfaces
 
can
 
produce
 
vacuum
 
polarization,
 
particle
 
creation,
 
and
 
divergent
 
stress–energy
 
in
 
semiclassical
 
treatments,
 
potentially
 
destabilizing
 
the
 
geometry.
 
In
 
black
 
hole
 
physics,
 
horizons
 
are
 
associated
 
with
 
Hawking
 
radiation.
 
In
 
warp
 
spacetimes,
 
analogous
 
effects
 
may
 
appear,
 
raising
 
the
 
possibility
 
that
 
quantum
 
backreaction
 
becomes
 
catastrophic.
 
[45]
 
[46]
 
[33]
 
 
7.6
 
Quantum
 
Instabilities
 
and
 
Chronology
 
Protection
 
Links
 
Another
 
deep
 
connection
 
between
 
warp
 
metrics
 
and
 
teleportation
 
arises
 
through
 
causality.
 
Superluminal
 
travel
 
in
 
general
 
relativity
 
can,
 
under
 
suitable
 
arrangements,
 
lead
 
to
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
(CTCs),
 
enabling
 
time
 
travel
 
and
 
causal
 
paradoxes.
 
Wormholes
 
share
 
this
 
issue;
 
warp
 
bubbles
 
do
 
as
 
well.
 
As
 
a
 
result,
 
one
 
must
 
consider
 
whether
 
the
 
laws
 
of
 
physics
 
enforce
 
a
 
“chronology
 
protection”
 
principle
 
that
 
prevents
 
macroscopic
 
time
 
machines
 
from
 
forming.
 
If
 
such
 
protection
 
exists,
 
warp
 
spacetimes
 
may
 
be
 
inherently
 
unstable
 
once
 
they
 
approach
 
configurations
 
that
 
allow
 
CTCs.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
[49]
 
Semiclassical
 
arguments
 
suggest
 
that
 
vacuum
 
fluctuations
 
may
 
become
 
arbitrarily
 
large
 
near
 
would-be
 
CTC
 
formation
 
regions,
 
generating
 
diverging
 
stress–energy
 
that
 
destroys
 
the
 
geometry
 
before
 
a
 
time
 
machine
 
forms.
 
While
 
these
 
arguments
 
are
 
not
 
definitive
 
(since
 
full
 
quantum
 
gravity
 
is
 
unknown),
 
they
 
provide
 
an
 
important
 
caution:
 
the
 
very
 
mechanism
 
that
 
enables
 
warp-like
 
effective
 
teleportation
 
may
 
simultaneously
 
trigger
 
instabilities
 
that
 
enforce
 
causal
 
consistency.
 
[8]
 
[33]
 
[53]
 
 
7.7
 
Comparison
 
to
 
Wormholes:
 
Advantages
 
and
 
Disadvantages

===== PAGE 32 =====
32
 
Warp/bubble
 
metrics
 
differ
 
from
 
wormholes
 
in
 
one
 
key
 
aspect
 
relevant
 
to
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe”:
 
a
 
wormhole
 
typically
 
requires
 
a
 
mouth
 
at
 
or
 
near
 
the
 
destination,
 
while
 
a
 
warp
 
bubble
 
can
 
in
 
principle
 
move
 
through
 
space-time
 
to
 
arbitrary
 
destinations
 
without
 
pre-deployed
 
infrastructure.
 
This
 
makes
 
warp-like
 
transport
 
appear
 
more
 
flexible
 
for
 
universe-wide
 
travel.
 
[13]
 
[22]
 
However,
 
this
 
advantage
 
is
 
offset
 
by
 
major
 
disadvantages:
 
1.
 
Energy
 
requirement:
 
warp
 
bubbles
 
often
 
require
 
extreme
 
integrated
 
negative
 
energy.
 
[54]
 
2.
 
Control
 
problem:
 
superluminal
 
bubbles
 
may
 
be
 
unsteerable
 
from
 
inside.
 
[49]
 
3.

[33]

[8]

8.1
 
The
 
Vacuum

In

9.
 
Topological
 
Space-Time
 
Surgery
 
and
 
Nontrivial
 
Connectivity
 
The
 
teleportation
 
of
 
macroscopic
 
matter
 
to
 
arbitrary
 
locations
 
in
 
the
 
universe
 
can
 
be

as
 
a

then

between

ordinary

separation.
 
Wormholes
 
provide
 
one

this

but

typically

fixed

A

that

of
 
space-time

be

creating
 
new
 
connections
 
or
 
identifications
 
between
 
regions.
 
This

often
 
described
 
as
 
topological

in

the
 
global

of

is

Such

exotic
 
stress–energy

or

the
 
underlying

of

so
 
that
 
distant
 
regions

topologically
 
adjacent.
 
[14]
 
[16]
 
[23]
 
 
9.1
 
Topology
 
in
 
General
 
Relativity:
 
The
 
Basic
 
Language
 
Topology
 
concerns
 
properties
 
of
 
space
 
that
 
remain
 
invariant
 
under
 
continuous
 
deformations.
 
In
 
general
 
relativity,
 
the
 
universe
 
is
 
represented
 
by
 
a
 
smooth
 
manifold
 
M
 
with
 
a
 
Lorentzian
 
metric
 
g
μ
v
 
The
 
metric
 
determines
 
local
 
geometry
 
(distances,
 
angles,
 
causal
 
structure),
 
while
 
the
 
manifold
 
topology
 
determines
 
the
 
global
 
connectivity.
 
Two
 
spacetimes
 
may
 
have
 
identical
 
local
 
curvature
 
in
 
many
 
regions
 
but
 
differ
 
fundamentally
 
in
 
global
 
topology.
 
For
 
example,
 
ordinary
 
flat
 
space
 
R³
 
and
 
a
 
three-torus
 
T³
  
can
 
locally
 
look
 
Euclidean
 
yet
 
differ
 
in
 
global
 
identification
 
of
 
points.
 
[14]
 
[23]
 
From
 
a
 
teleportation
 
perspective,
 
topology
 
becomes
 
relevant
 
because
 
connectivity
 
determines
 
whether
 
there
 
exist
 
globally
 
short
 
paths
 
between
 
far-separated
 
coordinate
 
regions.
 
If
 
the
 
manifold
 
contains
 
handles,
 
identifications,
 
or
 
nontrivial
 
fundamental
 
group,
 
a
 
traveler
 
might
 
reach
 
an
 
apparently
 
distant
 
location
 
by
 
passing
 
through
 
a
 
topological
 
shortcut.
 
Wormholes
 
themselves
 
may
 
be
 
interpreted
 
topologically
 
as
 
adding
 
a
 
handle
 
connecting
 
two
 
regions.
 
Thus,
 
wormholes
 
sit
 
at
 
the
 
boundary
 
between
 
metric
 
engineering
 
and
 
topology
 
engineering.
 
[21]
 
[22]
 
 
9.2
 
Topology
 
Change
 
and
 
the
 
Geroch
 
Theorem
 
A
 
key
 
question
 
is
 
whether
 
topology
 
can
 
change
 
dynamically
 
in
 
classical
 
general
 
relativity.
 
If
 
one
 
begins
 
with
 
a
 
spacetime
 
slice
 
of
 
one
 
topology
 
and
 
evolves
 
it
 
to
 
a
 
different
 
topology,
 
this
 
would
 
represent
 
a
 
true
 
topological
 
transformation.
 
However,
 
there
 
are
 
strong
 
restrictions.
 
Geroch’s
 
results
 
imply
 
that
 
in
 
classical
 
general
 
relativity,
 
smooth
 
topology
 
change
 
in
 
a
 
globally
 
hyperbolic
 
spacetime
 
is
 
not
 
possible
 
without
 
encountering
 
pathologies
 
such
 
as
 
singularities
 
or
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves.
 
In
 
simplified
 
terms,
 
topology
 
change
 
tends
 
to
 
force
 
either:
 
1.
 
Singular
 
behavior,
 
meaning
 
curvature
 
blow-up
 
or
 
incomplete
 
geodesics,
 
or
 
2.
 
Causality
 
violation,
 
meaning
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
or
 
failure
 
of
 
global
 
hyperbolicity.
 
[14]
 
[65]
 
Global
 
hyperbolicity
 
is
 
the
 
condition
 
that
 
ensures
 
well-posed
 
evolution
 
from
 
initial
 
data
 
on
 
a
 
Cauchy
 
surface.
 
It
 
excludes
 
CTCs
 
and
 
many
 
causality-violating
 
spacetimes.
 
If
 
topology
 
change
 
requires
 
violation
 
of
 
global
 
hyperbolicity,
 
then
 
the
 
classical
 
evolution
 
laws
 
of
 
GR
 
lose
 
their
 
predictive
 
structure.
 
This
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
a
 
philosophical
 
issue;
 
it
 
is
 
a
 
mathematical
 
obstruction
 
to
 
using
 
classical
 
GR
 
as
 
an
 
“engineering
 
theory”
 
for
 
topology
 
change.
 
[23]
 
[14]

===== PAGE 38 =====
38
 
Thus,
 
topological
 
surgery
 
in
 
classical
 
GR
 
appears
 
to
 
be
 
fundamentally
 
constrained.
 
Teleportation
 
via
 
topology
 
change
 
would
 
either
 
require
 
accepting
 
singularities
 
(which
 
are
 
physically
 
destructive)
 
or
 
accepting
 
causality
 
violations
 
(which
 
create
 
paradoxes),
 
or
 
abandoning
 
the
 
assumption
 
of
 
classical
 
global
 
hyperbolicity.
 
[14]
 
[8]
 
 
9.3
 
Morse
 
Theory,
 
Cobordism,
 
and
 
“Surgery”
 
Viewpoint
 
Topological
 
surgery
 
is
 
often
 
described
 
mathematically
 
using
 
the
 
language
 
of
 
cobordism
 
and
 
Morse
 
theory.
 
While
 
a
 
full
 
technical
 
treatment
 
is
 
beyond
 
this
 
thesis
 
scope,
 
the
 
conceptual
 
picture
 
is
 
as
 
follows:
 
two
 
spatial
 
topologies
 
can
 
be
 
connected
 
by
 
a
 
four-dimensional
 
interpolating
 
spacetime
 
manifold
 
whose
 
topology
 
encodes
 
the
 
transformation.
 
The
 
topology
 
change
 
occurs
 
at
 
critical
 
points
 
where
 
the
 
manifold’s
 
structure
 
cannot
 
be
 
represented
 
as
 
a
 
smooth
 
product
 
of
 
time
 
with
 
a
 
fixed
 
spatial
 
slice.
 
These
 
critical
 
points
 
are
 
typically
 
associated
 
with
 
degeneracy
 
in
 
the
 
metric
 
or
 
with
 
regions
 
where
 
classical
 
descriptions
 
break
 
down.
 
[66]
 
[67]
 
This
 
viewpoint
 
reinforces
 
the
 
earlier
 
conclusion:
 
topology
 
change
 
is
 
not
 
a
 
smooth,
 
ordinary
 
dynamical
 
evolution
 
in
 
classical
 
GR.
 
It
 
is
 
more
 
naturally
 
associated
 
with
 
quantum
 
gravity
 
regimes,
 
where
 
spacetime
 
may
 
not
 
be
 
smooth
 
and
 
classical
 
causality
 
may
 
not
 
be
 
fundamental.
 
 
9.4
 
The
 
Need
 
for
 
Quantum
 
Gravity
 
If
 
topology
 
change
 
is
 
obstructed
 
in
 
classical
 
GR,
 
then
 
any
 
realistic
 
topological
 
teleportation
 
mechanism
 
must
 
involve
 
physics
 
beyond
 
the
 
classical
 
continuum
 
description.
 
Quantum
 
gravity
 
is
 
expected
 
to
 
modify
 
spacetime
 
structure
 
at
 
extremely
 
small
 
scales,
 
especially
 
near
 
the
 
Planck
 
length:
 
 
At
 
this
 
scale,
 
quantum
 
fluctuations
 
of
 
geometry
 
may
 
become
 
significant,
 
leading
 
to
 
a
 
picture
 
in
 
which
 
spacetime
 
topology
 
is
 
not
 
fixed
 
but
 
fluctuates.
 
Wheeler’s
 
concept
 
of
 
quantum
 
foam
 
describes
 
a
 
spacetime
 
at
 
Planck
 
scales
 
containing
 
transient
 
wormholes
 
and
 
topological
 
fluctuations.
 
If
 
such
 
foam
 
exists,
 
then
 
topology
 
change
 
could
 
occur
 
naturally
 
at
 
microscopic
 
scales.
 
The
 
challenge
 
for
 
teleportation
 
would
 
then
 
be
 
to
 
enlarge,
 
stabilize,
 
and
 
control
 
these
 
fluctuations
 
to
 
macroscopic
 
scales.
 
[16]
 
[68]
 
[69]
 
Thus,
 
topological
 
space-time
 
surgery
 
connects
 
directly
 
to
 
the
 
quantum
 
foam
 
concept:
 
topological
 
shortcuts
 
may
 
already
 
exist
 
at
 
microscopic
 
levels,
 
but
 
they
 
are
 
unstable
 
and
 
inaccessible.
 
A
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
would
 
require
 
control
 
over
 
quantum
 
gravity
 
degrees
 
of
 
freedom,
 
enabling
 
the
 
conversion
 
of
 
microscopic
 
topology
 
into
 
a
 
stable
 
macroscopic
 
channel.
 
This
 
is
 
far
 
beyond
 
current
 
physics,
 
but
 
it
 
provides
 
a
 
logically
 
consistent
 
pathway
 
linking
 
topology
 
manipulation
 
and
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation.
 
[16]
 
[22]
 
 
9.5
 
Energy
 
Conditions
 
and
 
Topology
 
Censorship
 
Even
 
when
 
topology
 
does
 
not
 
change
 
dynamically,
 
one
 
can
 
ask
 
whether
 
nontrivial
 
topology
 
can
 
be
 
traversable
 
or
 
“visible”
 
to
 
observers.
 
The
 
topological
 
censorship
 
theorem
 
suggests
 
that
 
under
 
reasonable
 
assumptions—such
 
as
 
the
 
averaged
 
null
 
energy
 
condition
 
(ANEC)
 
and
 
asymptotic
 
flatness—nontrivial
 
topology
 
cannot
 
be
 
traversed
 
by
 
causal
 
curves;
 
it
 
is
 
effectively
 
hidden
 
behind
 
horizons.
 
In
 
other
 
words,
 
even
 
if
 
spacetime
 
contains
 
nontrivial
 
topology,
 
classical
 
energy
 
conditions
 
can
 
prevent
 
travelers
 
from
 
exploiting
 
it
 
to
 
achieve
 
shortcuts.
 
[70]
 
[71]
 
This
 
theorem
 
reinforces
 
the
 
central
 
theme:
 
exploiting
 
topology
 
for
 
teleportation
 
tends
 
to
 
require
 
energy
 
condition
 
violations.
 
Traversable
 
topological
 
shortcuts,
 
like
 
traversable
 
wormholes,
 
demand

===== PAGE 39 =====
39
 
exotic
 
stress–energy.
 
Therefore,
 
topology-based
 
teleportation
 
does
 
not
 
escape
 
the
 
negative-energy
 
barrier;
 
rather,
 
it
 
reframes
 
it
 
in
 
global
 
terms.
 
[21]
 
[44]
 
 
9.6
 
Teleportation
 
Implications:
 
“Anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
Universe”
 
If
 
one
 
could
 
perform
 
genuine
 
topological
 
surgery,
 
then
 
the
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe”
 
requirement
 
could
 
be
 
satisfied
 
in
 
an
 
especially
 
direct
 
way:
 
one
 
might
 
create
 
a
 
new
 
topological
 
connection
 
between
 
the
 
origin
 
and
 
the
 
destination,
 
effectively
 
making
 
them
 
adjacent
 
in
 
the
 
manifold.
 
This
 
would
 
be
 
closer
 
to
 
instantaneous
 
teleportation
 
than
 
wormhole
 
traversal
 
in
 
the
 
usual
 
sense,
 
because
 
it
 
could
 
be
 
conceptualized
 
as
 
a
 
topology-level
 
identification
 
rather
 
than
 
a
 
finite-length
 
tunnel.
 
However,
 
such
 
operations
 
would
 
be
 
so
 
far
 
beyond
 
known
 
physics
 
that
 
they
 
likely
 
belong
 
to
 
a
 
regime
 
where
 
classical
 
concepts
 
of
 
distance
 
and
 
time
 
lose
 
their
 
meaning.
 
[16]
 
[66]
 
Additionally,
 
topology
 
change
 
is
 
entangled
 
with
 
causality.
 
If
 
one
 
can
 
freely
 
connect
 
arbitrary
 
regions,
 
one
 
can
 
likely
 
create
 
causal
 
loops
 
under
 
certain
 
configurations,
 
leading
 
to
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves.
 
Therefore,
 
even
 
if
 
topological
 
teleportation
 
were
 
possible,
 
it
 
would
 
likely
 
be
 
subject
 
to
 
chronology
 
protection
 
constraints
 
or
 
deep
 
consistency
 
requirements
 
in
 
quantum
 
gravity.
 
[8]
 
[23]
 
 
9.7
 
Summary:
 
Topology
 
Engineering
 
as
 
a
 
Deep,
 
Quantum-Gravity-Dependent
 
Route
 
Topological
 
space-time
 
surgery
 
represents
 
a
 
deeply
 
fundamental
 
pathway
 
toward
 
teleportation
 
because
 
it
 
targets
 
the
 
global
 
connectivity
 
structure
 
of
 
the
 
universe
 
rather
 
than
 
local
 
metric
 
distortions.
 
Classical
 
GR
 
strongly
 
restricts
 
topology
 
change
 
through
 
results
 
indicating
 
that
 
topology
 
change
 
implies
 
singularities
 
or
 
causality
 
violations.
 
Furthermore,
 
even
 
pre-existing
 
nontrivial
 
topology
 
tends
 
to
 
be
 
censored
 
under
 
energy
 
conditions.
 
Therefore,
 
topology-based
 
teleportation
 
likely
 
requires
 
quantum
 
gravity,
 
where
 
spacetime
 
topology
 
may
 
fluctuate
 
at
 
Planck
 
scales
 
and
 
could,
 
in
 
principle,
 
be
 
manipulated.
 
This
 
connects
 
naturally
 
to
 
the
 
next
 
chapter,
 
which
 
investigates
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
(CTCs)
 
as
 
another
 
consequence
 
of
 
exotic
 
connectivity
 
and
 
superluminal
 
transport.
 
CTCs
 
are
 
both
 
a
 
potential
 
mechanism
 
for
 
unusual
 
transport
 
and
 
a
 
central
 
threat
 
to
 
causal
 
consistency,
 
making
 
them
 
essential
 
for
 
evaluating
 
the
 
feasibility
 
of
 
universe-wide
 
teleportation
 
technologies.
 
[14]
 
[8]
 
[16]
 
[70]
 
 
10.
 
Closed
 
Timelike
 
Curves
 
(CTCs)
 
and
 
CTC-Assisted
 
Transport
 
Closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
(CTCs)
 
are
 
among
 
the
 
most
 
controversial
 
structures
 
permitted
 
by
 
the
 
mathematics
 
of
 
general
 
relativity.
 
A
 
CTC
 
is
 
a
 
worldline
 
that
 
is
 
timelike
 
everywhere—meaning
 
a
 
massive
 
object
 
could
 
travel
 
along
 
it
 
locally
 
slower
 
than
 
light—yet
 
it
 
loops
 
back
 
to
 
intersect
 
its
 
own
 
past.
 
In
 
such
 
a
 
spacetime,
 
an
 
observer
 
could
 
in
 
principle
 
return
 
to
 
an
 
earlier
 
event
 
in
 
their
 
own
 
timeline,
 
creating
 
the
 
possibility
 
of
 
time
 
travel
 
and
 
the
 
well-known
 
causality
 
paradoxes.
 
For
 
a
 
thesis
 
focused
 
on
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe,”
 
CTCs
 
appear
 
in
 
two
 
different
 
roles.
 
First,
 
they
 
can
 
be
 
interpreted
 
as
 
a
 
kind
 
of
 
transport
 
mechanism:
 
if
 
causal
 
loops
 
exist,
 
then
 
unusual
 
paths
 
might
 
connect
 
distant
 
events
 
in
 
apparently
 
non-classical
 
ways.
 
Second,
 
and
 
more
 
importantly,
 
CTCs
 
arise
 
as
 
a
 
byproduct
 
of
 
superluminal
 
or
 
shortcut
 
transport
 
mechanisms
 
such
 
as
 
wormholes
 
and
 
warp
 
drives.
 
Thus,
 
any
 
advanced
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
must
 
confront
 
whether
 
CTCs
 
can
 
form
 
and
 
whether
 
physical
 
laws
 
prevent
 
their
 
formation
 
through
 
a
 
chronology
 
protection
 
mechanism.
 
[8]
 
[15]
 
[47]
 
[53]
 
 
10.1
 
Definition:
 
What
 
Exactly
 
Is
 
a
 
Closed
 
Timelike
 
Curve?
 
Let
 
(
 
M,g
μ
v
)
 
be
 
a
 
spacetime
 
manifold
 
with
 
Lorentzian
 
metric.
 
A
 
curve
 
γ(λ)
 
is
 
timelike
 
if
 
its
 
tangent
 
vector
 
u
μ
 
=
 
dx
μ
/d
λ

===== PAGE 40 =====
40
 
 
(assuming
 
(
 
-
 
+
 
+
 
+
 
)
 
signature).
 
The
 
curve
 
is
 
closed
 
if
 
there
 
exist
 
parameters
 
λ
2
 
<
 
λ
2
 
such
 
that:
 
 
γ(λ
1
)
 
=
 
γ(λ
2
),
 
 
meaning
 
the
 
curve
 
returns
 
to
 
the
 
same
 
spacetime
 
event.
 
A
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curve
 
therefore
 
satisfies
 
both
 
timelike
 
and
 
closed
 
conditions.
 
Importantly,
 
no
 
local
 
physical
 
law
 
is
 
violated
 
along
 
the
 
curve:
 
locally,
 
the
 
traveler
 
moves
 
within
 
their
 
light
 
cone.
 
The
 
violation
 
is
 
global:
 
the
 
causal
 
structure
 
of
 
spacetime
 
permits
 
loops
 
in
 
time.
 
[23]
 
[3]
 
CTCs
 
imply
 
failure
 
of
 
global
 
causality
 
conditions
 
such
 
as
 
chronology
 
and
 
global
 
hyperbolicity.
 
A
 
spacetime
 
is
 
chronological
 
if
 
it
 
contains
 
no
 
CTCs.
 
A
 
spacetime
 
is
 
globally
 
hyperbolic
 
if
 
it
 
admits
 
Cauchy
 
surfaces
 
that
 
determine
 
deterministic
 
evolution.
 
Teleportation
 
systems
 
that
 
allow
 
effective
 
superluminal
 
travel
 
may
 
threaten
 
these
 
conditions.
 
[23]
 
[8]
 
 
10.2
 
Gödel
 
Spacetime:
 
A
 
Classical
 
Example
 
of
 
CTCs
 
Gödel
 
provided
 
one
 
of
 
the
 
earliest
 
explicit
 
solutions
 
to
 
Einstein’s
 
field
 
equations
 
that
 
contains
 
CTCs.
 
The
 
Gödel
 
universe
 
is
 
a
 
rotating
 
cosmological
 
solution
 
with
 
nontrivial
 
global
 
structure.
 
Without
 
requiring
 
superluminal
 
local
 
motion,
 
it
 
allows
 
timelike
 
curves
 
that
 
wrap
 
around
 
and
 
return
 
to
 
earlier
 
times.
 
The
 
Gödel
 
solution
 
demonstrates
 
a
 
crucial
 
point:
 
CTCs
 
are
 
not
 
merely
 
artifacts
 
of
 
exotic
 
matter;
 
they
 
can
 
exist
 
in
 
solutions
 
with
 
ordinary
 
(though
 
cosmologically
 
unrealistic)
 
matter
 
content.
 
[15]
 
[23]
 
For
 
teleportation
 
studies,
 
Gödel
 
spacetime
 
is
 
often
 
treated
 
as
 
a
 
conceptual
 
warning:
 
the
 
field
 
equations
 
of
 
GR
 
do
 
not
 
automatically
 
enforce
 
global
 
causality.
 
Thus,
 
if
 
teleportation
 
mechanisms
 
rely
 
on
 
extreme
 
geometries,
 
one
 
must
 
actively
 
analyze
 
causal
 
structure
 
and
 
determine
 
whether
 
the
 
system
 
inevitably
 
generates
 
CTC
 
regions.
 
[8]
 
[23]

10:

10.3
 
Wormholes
 
as
 
Time
 
Machines:
 
The
 
Morris–Thorne–Yurtsever
 
Mechanism
 
Traversable
 
wormholes
 
can
 
be
 
converted
 
into
 
time
 
machines
 
under
 
plausible
 
manipulations.
 
Morris,
 
Thorne,
 
and
 
Yurtsever
 
showed
 
that
 
if
 
two
 
wormhole
 
mouths
 
acquire
 
a
 
time
 
shift
 
relative
 
to
 
one
 
another—either
 
by
 
accelerating
 
one
 
mouth
 
to
 
relativistic
 
speeds
 
and
 
returning
 
it,
 
or
 
by
 
placing
 
it
 
in
 
a
 
different
 
gravitational
 
potential—then
 
a
 
traveler
 
can
 
traverse
 
the
 
wormhole
 
and
 
emerge
 
at
 
an
 
earlier
 
external
 
time.
 
This
 
process
 
is
 
analogous
 
to
 
the
 
twin
 
paradox
 
in
 
special
 
relativity:
 
differential
 
time
 
dilation
 
creates
 
a
 
desynchronization
 
between
 
the
 
mouths.
 
[47]
 
[4]
 
If
 
the
 
time
 
shift
 
becomes
 
large
 
enough,
 
then
 
a
 
traveler
 
can
 
go
 
through
 
the
 
wormhole
 
and
 
return
 
through
 
external
 
space
 
in
 
such
 
a
 
way
 
that
 
they
 
arrive
 
before
 
they
 
departed,
 
forming
 
a
 
CTC.
 
This
 
is
 
not
 
an
 
exotic
 
add-on;
 
it
 
is
 
a
 
direct
 
consequence
 
of
 
combining
 
wormhole
 
shortcuts
 
with
 
relativistic
 
time
 
dilation.
 
Therefore,
 
wormhole
 
teleportation
 
and
 
time
 
travel
 
are
 
tightly
 
coupled
 
in
 
the
 
physics
 
of
 
GR.
 
[47]
 
[8]
 
This
 
coupling
 
is
 
central
 
for
 
a
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe.”
 
If
 
a
 
wormhole
 
network
 
exists
 
with
 
multiple
 
mouths,
 
relative
 
motions
 
and
 
gravitational
 
time
 
dilation
 
among
 
nodes
 
can
 
easily
 
create
 
time
 
shifts.
 
In
 
a
 
sufficiently
 
complex
 
network,
 
loops
 
might
 
generate
 
CTCs
 
even
 
unintentionally.
 
This
 
means
 
a
 
functioning
 
teleportation
 
network
 
could
 
be
 
constrained
 
not
 
only
 
by
 
negative
 
energy
 
requirements
 
but
 
also
 
by
 
causality
 
enforcement
 
constraints.
 
[53]
 
[22]
 
 
10.4
 
Warp
 
Drives
 
and
 
CTC
 
Construction

===== PAGE 42 =====
42
 
Warp
 
drive
 
spacetimes
 
can
 
also
 
lead
 
to
 
causality
 
violations.
 
Superluminal
 
travel
 
between
 
two
 
points
 
can
 
be
 
used,
 
in
 
principle,
 
to
 
send
 
signals
 
into
 
the
 
past
 
from
 
some
 
frames,
 
depending
 
on
 
the
 
global
 
arrangement
 
of
 
trajectories.
 
The
 
basic
 
reasoning
 
resembles
 
the
 
tachyon
 
paradox
 
in
 
special
 
relativity:
 
faster-than-light
 
communication
 
can
 
be
 
combined
 
with
 
Lorentz
 
transformations
 
to
 
create
 
closed
 
causal
 
loops.
 
In
 
GR,
 
warp
 
travel
 
effectively
 
creates
 
global
 
superluminal
 
transport,
 
and
 
in
 
certain
 
configurations
 
it
 
can
 
generate
 
CTCs.
 
[49]
 
[54]
 
[8]
 
Therefore,
 
warp-based
 
teleportation
 
is
 
not
 
only
 
a
 
negative
 
energy
 
problem;
 
it
 
is
 
also
 
a
 
causality
 
problem.
 
Even
 
if
 
one
 
could
 
generate
 
the
 
stress–energy
 
needed
 
for
 
a
 
warp
 
bubble,
 
preventing
 
causal
 
paradoxes
 
might
 
be
 
impossible
 
without
 
an
 
additional
 
consistency
 
principle.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
 
10.5
 
Chronology
 
Protection
 
Conjecture
 
Hawking
 
proposed
 
the
 
chronology
 
protection
 
conjecture,
 
the
 
idea
 
that
 
the
 
laws
 
of
 
physics
 
prevent
 
the
 
formation
 
of
 
CTCs
 
on
 
macroscopic
 
scales.
 
The
 
motivation
 
is
 
that
 
time
 
machines
 
create
 
paradoxes,
 
threatening
 
predictability.
 
Hawking
 
suggested
 
that
 
quantum
 
effects
 
may
 
become
 
arbitrarily
 
large
 
near
 
the
 
formation
 
of
 
a
 
chronology
 
horizon,
 
producing
 
divergent
 
stress–energy
 
that
 
destroys
 
the
 
would-be
 
CTC
 
region
 
before
 
it
 
can
 
form.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
While
 
a
 
full
 
proof
 
of
 
chronology
 
protection
 
requires
 
quantum
 
gravity,
 
semiclassical
 
analyses
 
support
 
the
 
idea
 
that
 
vacuum
 
fluctuations
 
can
 
diverge
 
as
 
one
 
approaches
 
conditions
 
enabling
 
CTCs.
 
Kim
 
and
 
Thorne
 
analyzed
 
whether
 
vacuum
 
fluctuations
 
prevent
 
CTC
 
formation
 
in
 
wormhole
 
configurations,
 
finding
 
that
 
they
 
may
 
become
 
large,
 
though
 
the
 
outcome
 
depends
 
on
 
assumptions
 
and
 
approximations.
 
The
 
general
 
lesson
 
is
 
that
 
quantum
 
backreaction
 
likely
 
plays
 
a
 
decisive
 
role
 
in
 
enforcing
 
causality,
 
and
 
thus
 
teleportation
 
mechanisms
 
that
 
approach
 
time-machine
 
regimes
 
may
 
trigger
 
self-destruction.
 
[53]
 
[33]
 
 
10.6
 
Paradoxes
 
and
 
Consistency
 
Conditions
 
CTCs
 
create
 
logical
 
paradoxes
 
such
 
as
 
the
 
grandfather
 
paradox
 
and
 
the
 
information
 
paradox
 
(“bootstrap
 
paradox,”
 
where
 
information
 
appears
 
without
 
origin).
 
In
 
physics
 
literature,
 
these
 
paradoxes
 
motivate
 
consistency
 
conditions.
 
One
 
approach
 
is
 
the
 
Novikov
 
self-consistency
 
principle,
 
which
 
requires
 
that
 
events
 
on
 
a
 
CTC
 
be
 
globally
 
self-consistent.
 
Another
 
is
 
that
 
quantum
 
mechanics
 
may
 
enforce
 
consistency
 
by
 
selecting
 
histories
 
that
 
avoid
 
paradoxical
 
outcomes.
 
These
 
are
 
interpretive
 
frameworks
 
rather
 
than
 
confirmed
 
physical
 
laws,
 
but
 
they
 
reflect
 
the
 
deep
 
conceptual
 
instability
 
that
 
CTCs
 
introduce
 
into
 
physical
 
theory.
 
[72]
 
[73]
 
From
 
an
 
engineering
 
standpoint,
 
even
 
if
 
paradoxes
 
could
 
be
 
avoided
 
by
 
consistency
 
principles,
 
a
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
that
 
allows
 
time
 
travel
 
would
 
create
 
catastrophic
 
security
 
and
 
control
 
problems.
 
A
 
civilization-scale
 
teleportation
 
network
 
would
 
likely
 
require
 
built-in
 
causality
 
safeguards.
 
Thus,
 
CTC
 
analysis
 
is
 
not
 
optional;
 
it
 
is
 
part
 
of
 
any
 
realistic
 
assessment
 
of
 
universe-wide
 
teleportation.
 
[8]
 
[22]
 
 
10.7
 
CTC-Assisted
 
“Jump”
 
as
 
Teleportation
 
Mechanism
 
One
 
can
 
ask
 
whether
 
CTCs
 
can
 
be
 
used
 
directly
 
as
 
a
 
teleportation
 
mechanism—essentially
 
allowing
 
a
 
traveler
 
to
 
“jump”
 
not
 
only
 
across
 
space
 
but
 
also
 
across
 
time.
 
In
 
principle,
 
if
 
a
 
CTC
 
structure
 
connects
 
events
 
with
 
large
 
spatial
 
separation,
 
traversal
 
could
 
produce
 
a
 
combined
 
space-time
 
teleportation.
 
However,
 
this
 
is
 
not
 
a
 
separate
 
mechanism
 
from
 
wormholes
 
or
 
warp
 
systems;
 
it
 
is
 
usually
 
a
 
consequence
 
of
 
those
 
systems
 
arranged
 
in
 
a
 
causality-violating
 
manner.
 
Therefore,
 
CTC-assisted
 
transport
 
is
 
best
 
understood
 
as
 
an
 
emergent
 
extreme
 
regime
 
of
 
exotic
 
teleportation
 
rather
 
than
 
a
 
standalone
 
technology.
 
[47]
 
[49]
 
[8]

===== PAGE 43 =====
43
 
10.8
 
Summary:
 
CTCs
 
as
 
the
 
Primary
 
Causality
 
Barrier
 
Closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
represent
 
the
 
strongest
 
challenge
 
to
 
the
 
physical
 
plausibility
 
of
 
teleportation
 
systems.
 
They
 
demonstrate
 
that
 
exotic
 
geometries
 
enabling
 
rapid
 
transport
 
can
 
easily
 
lead
 
to
 
time
 
travel
 
and
 
causality
 
paradoxes.
 
Chronology
 
protection
 
arguments
 
suggest
 
that
 
quantum
 
effects
 
may
 
destabilize
 
any
 
system
 
that
 
approaches
 
CTC
 
formation,
 
potentially
 
forbidding
 
macroscopic
 
time
 
machines.
 
Thus,
 
any
 
technology
 
capable
 
of
 
teleporting
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
and
 
large
 
matter
 
anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe
 
must
 
either
 
operate
 
within
 
strict
 
causal
 
constraints
 
or
 
possess
 
a
 
new
 
physical
 
principle
 
that
 
prevents
 
paradoxical
 
global
 
structures.
 
The
 
next
 
chapter
 
explores
 
quantum
 
foam
 
shortcuts,
 
which
 
represent
 
a
 
different
 
approach:
 
instead
 
of
 
large
 
engineered
 
wormholes
 
or
 
warp
 
bubbles,
 
one
 
asks
 
whether

11.4
 
Why
 
Quantum
 
Foam
 
Does
 
Not
 
Automatically
 
Enable
 
Teleportation
 
It
 
is
 
crucial
 
not
 
to
 
exaggerate
 
what
 
quantum
 
foam
 
implies.
 
The

of
 
microscopic
 
topological
 
fluctuations
 
does
 
not
 
automatically
 
enable
 
macroscopic
 
travel.
 
Three
 
obstacles
 
dominate:
 
 
1.
 
Scale

Planck-scale
 
structures
 
are
 
10³
⁵
  
times
 
smaller
 
than
 
human-scale
 
distances.
 
2.
 
Stability:
 
foam
 
wormholes
 
are
 
transient
 
and
 
likely
 
decay
 
on
 
Planck
 
timescales.
 
3.
 
Control:
 
no
 
known
 
mechanism
 
exists
 
for
 
selecting,
 
amplifying,
 
or
 
positioning
 
microscopic
 
wormholes.
 
Even
 
if

micro-wormhole
 
exists,

to
 
a
 
radius
 
r
0
 
~
 
1
 
–
 
10
 
meters

by
 
a

of
 
10³
⁵
 
–
 
10
36
,
 
a

enormous

and

control.
 
Without

energy

at

appears
 
impossible.
 
Therefore,

does
 
not
 
remove

wormhole-like

11.5
 
Quantum
 
Foam
 
as
 
a
 
Precursor
 
to
 
Wormholes
 
and
 
Topology
 
Surgery
 
Quantum
 
foam
 
shortcuts
 
form
 
a
 
conceptual
 
bridge
 
between
 
two
 
earlier
 
themes:
 
topological
 
space-time
 
surgery
 
and
 
traversable
 
wormholes.
 
Classical
 
topology
 
surgery
 
is
 
obstructed
 
by
 
singularities
 
or
 
CTC
 
formation.
 
Quantum
 
foam
 
suggests
 
that
 
topology
 
change
 
may
 
occur
 
naturally
 
at
 
microscopic
 
scales,
 
bypassing
 
classical
 
obstructions
 
because
 
classical
 
smoothness
 
and
 
global
 
hyperbolicity
 
need
 
not
 
apply.
 
If
 
this
 
is
 
correct,
 
then
 
topology
 
engineering
 
could
 
proceed
 
by
 
manipulating
 
foam-level
 
processes
 
rather
 
than
 
performing
 
classical
 
topology
 
change.
 
This
 
creates
 
a
 
unified
 
picture:
 
●
 
Wormholes
 
are
 
macroscopic,
 
stabilized
 
topological
 
handles
 
in
 
spacetime.
 
●
 
Quantum
 
foam
 
is
 
microscopic,
 
fluctuating
 
handles.
 
●
 
Topology
 
surgery
 
is
 
the
 
idea
 
of
 
creating
 
or
 
modifying
 
handles
 
globally.
 
In
 
principle,
 
a
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
could
 
begin
 
with
 
foam-level
 
micro-handles
 
and
 
then
 
attempt
 
to
 
stabilize
 
and
 
enlarge
 
them
 
into
 
wormholes.
 
[16]
 
[67]
 
[69]

12.

Behind

All

field

the

T
μ
v
 
(or

⟨
 
T̂
μ
v
 
⟩
 
)

and

not

are

geometry
 
targets

different

configurations.
 
In
 
this
 
unified
 
framework,

anywhere

a
 
controllable
 
metric
 
that
 
connects
 
arbitrary
 
regions
 
through

and

12.3
 
Effective
 
Field
 
Theory
 
and
 
the
 
Limits
 
of
 
Control
 
Modern
 
physics
 
often
 
treats
 
gravitational
 
and
 
matter
 
dynamics
 
through
 
effective
 
field
 
theory
 
(EFT),
 
which
 
assumes
 
that
 
at
 
accessible
 
energies,
 
only
 
certain
 
degrees
 
of
 
freedom
 
are
 
relevant,
 
while
 
unknown
 
high-energy
 
physics
 
is
 
captured
 
by
 
higher-order
 
corrections.
 
In
 
the
 
EFT
 
viewpoint,
 
gravity
 
at
 
low
 
energies
 
is
 
described
 
by
 
Einstein’s
 
equations
 
plus
 
small
 
correction
 
terms
 
suppressed
 
by
 
high-energy
 
scales.
 
Under
 
this
 
assumption,
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
must
 
work
 
within
 
the
 
constraints
 
of
 
low-energy
 
EFT,
 
unless
 
it
 
operates
 
at
 
energies
 
so
 
high
 
that
 
quantum
 
gravity
 
becomes
 
dominant.
 
[76]
 
[77]
 
This
 
has
 
two
 
consequences:
 
1.
 
If
 
teleportation
 
is
 
attempted
 
at
 
macroscopic,
 
low-curvature
 
scales,
 
then
 
it
 
must
 
rely
 
on
 
semiclassical
 
violations
 
(negative
 
energy)
 
constrained
 
by
 
quantum
 
inequalities.
 
2.
 
If
 
teleportation
 
requires
 
Planck-scale
 
curvature
 
or
 
topology
 
change,
 
then
 
it
 
lies
 
beyond
 
EFT
 
and
 
demands
 
full
 
quantum
 
gravity.
 
Thus,
 
energy-to-geometry
 
conversion
 
becomes
 
a
 
scaling
 
problem:
 
either
 
one
 
achieves
 
extraordinary
 
stress–energy
 
control
 
at
 
low
 
curvature
 
(extremely
 
hard),
 
or
 
one
 
enters
 
a
 
regime
 
where
 
known
 
physics
 
becomes
 
incomplete.
 
[44]
 
[16]
 
[74]

body
 
length
 
scale.
 
Additionally,
 
stability
 
requires
 
that
 
small
 
perturbations
 
in
 
the
 
metric
 
and
 
stress–energy
 
do
 
not
 
rapidly
 
destroy
 
the
 
geometry.
 
In
 
many
 
wormhole
 
and
 
warp
 
constructions,
 
the
 
exotic
 
structure
 
is
 
not
 
stable
 
without
 
active
 
feedback—implying
 
that
 
teleportation
 
may
 
require
 
continuous
 
control
 
fields
 
rather
 
than
 
a
 
passive
 
device.
 
[4]
 
[21]
 
[50]
 
 
12.6
 
The
 
Causality
 
Constraint
 
as
 
a
 
Global
 
Safety
 
Condition
 
Perhaps
 
the
 
most
 
fundamental
 
non-negotiable
 
constraint
 
is
 
causality.
 
If
 
a
 
teleportation
 
device
 
allows
 
superluminal
 
transport
 
relative
 
to
 
distant
 
observers,
 
it
 
may
 
enable
 
the
 
formation
 
of
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves
 
(CTCs)
 
in
 
some
 
global
 
configurations,
 
threatening
 
consistency
 
and
 
predictability.
 
Therefore,
 
any
 
“teleport
 
anywhere”
 
technology
 
must
 
include
 
a
 
causality
 
constraint:
 
 
No
 
formation
 
of
 
CTCs
 
in
 
the
 
accessible
 
spacetime
 
region.
 
 
In
 
classical
 
GR,
 
this
 
constraint
 
is
 
not
 
automatically
 
guaranteed.
 
In
 
semiclassical
 
gravity,
 
chronology
 
protection
 
may
 
arise
 
through
 
divergent
 
vacuum
 
fluctuations.
 
In
 
quantum
 
gravity,
 
causality
 
constraints
 
may
 
be
 
enforced
 
more
 
deeply.
 
But
 
from
 
an
 
engineering
 
standpoint,
 
the
 
causality
 
constraint
 
functions

===== PAGE 49 =====
49
 
as
 
a
 
safety
 
requirement:
 
a
 
teleportation
 
system
 
must
 
not
 
create
 
paradoxical
 
global
 
structures
 
even
 
unintentionally.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
[47]
 
 
12.7
 
Summary:
 
Teleportation
 
as
 
Stress–Energy
 
Mastery
 
Energy-to-geometry
 
conversion
 
provides
 
the
 
cleanest
 
unifying
 
interpretation:
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
is
 
a
 
stress–energy
 
engineering
 
problem.
 
Wormholes
 
and
 
warp
 
drives
 
are
 
geometry
 
designs;
 
negative
 
energy
 
engineering
 
and
 
vacuum
 
manipulation
 
are
 
resource-generation
 
strategies;
 
topology
 
surgery
 
and
 
quantum
 
foam
 
represent
 
quantum-gravity-level
 
extensions;
 
CTCs
 
represent
 
causality
 
failure
 
modes.
 
Therefore,
 
the
 
feasibility
 
of
 
teleportation
 
depends
 
on
 
whether
 
future
 
physics
 
and
 
technology
 
can
 
achieve
 
the
 
following:
 
1.
 
Controlled
 
NEC
 
violation
 
in
 
localized
 
regions.
 
2.
 
Stable
 
maintenance
 
of
 
exotic
 
geometry
 
without
 
collapse.
 
3.
 
Human
 
survivability
 
against
 
tidal
 
forces
 
and
 
quantum
 
instabilities.
 
4.
 
Global
 
causality
 
preservation.
 
The
 
next
 
chapter
 
provides
 
a
 
unified
 
feasibility
 
ranking
 
of
 
all
 
eight
 
mechanisms
 
and
 
identifies
 
which
 
pathway
 
is
 
most
 
promising
 
for
 
eventual
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation,
 
given
 
known
 
constraints.
 
[21]
 
[22]
 
[44]
 
 
13.
 
Unified
 
Comparison
 
and
 
Feasibility
 
Ranking
 
of
 
Exotic
 
Teleportation
 
Concepts
 
A
 
thesis
 
that
 
aims
 
to
 
discuss
 
a
 
technology
 
capable
 
of
 
teleporting
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
and
 
large
 
matter
 
anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe
 
must
 
ultimately
 
answer
 
a
 
practical
 
scientific
 
question:
 
among
 
the
 
candidate
 
concepts,
 
which
 
pathway
 
is
 
most
 
promising
 
in
 
principle,
 
and
 
what
 
are
 
the
 
decisive
 
obstacles?
 
The
 
preceding
 
chapters
 
established
 
that
 
general
 
relativity
 
and
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
permit
 
exotic
 
geometries
 
and
 
negative-energy
 
phenomena,
 
but
 
they
 
also
 
impose
 
severe
 
constraints
 
through
 
energy
 
conditions,
 
quantum
 
inequalities,
 
and
 
causal
 
consistency
 
requirements.
 
This
 
chapter
 
presents
 
a
 
unified
 
comparative
 
framework
 
for
 
the
 
eight
 
concepts,
 
using
 
consistent
 
evaluation
 
criteria
 
rooted
 
in
 
mathematical
 
consistency,
 
physical
 
constraints,
 
and
 
survivability
 
requirements
 
for
 
macroscopic
 
matter.
 
[21]
 
[22]
 
[44]
 
[49]
 
 
13.1
 
Evaluation
 
Criteria
 
for
 
Macroscopic
 
Teleportation
 
To
 
compare
 
different
 
teleportation
 
paradigms
 
rigorously,
 
the
 
thesis
 
applies
 
the
 
following
 
criteria:
 
1.
 
Mathematical
 
Consistency
 
(MC):
 
Whether
 
the
 
concept
 
has
 
a
 
well-defined
 
mathematical
 
formulation
 
in
 
GR/QFT
 
and
 
avoids
 
internal
 
contradictions
 
at
 
the
 
level
 
of
 
equations.
 
[2]
 
[3]
 
2.
 
Stress–Energy
 
Realizability
 
(SER):
 
Whether
 
the
 
required
 
T
μ
v
 
 
can
 
plausibly
 
arise
 
from
 
known
 
or
 
minimally
 
extended
 
physical
 
fields,
 
including
 
whether
 
NEC
 
violation
 
is
 
required.
 
[21]
 
[44]
 
3.
 
Energy
 
Scale
 
Feasibility
 
(ESF):
 
Whether
 
the
 
integrated
 
energy
 
requirements
 
are
 
within
 
conceivable
 
bounds
 
for
 
advanced
 
civilizations,
 
and
 
how
 
they
 
scale
 
with
 
throat
 
radius
 
r
0
,
 
bubble
 
radius
 
R,
 
or
 
Planck-scale
 
parameters.
 
[54]
 
[21]
 
4.
 
Stability
 
Under
 
Perturbations
 
(SUP):
 
Whether
 
the
 
geometry
 
can
 
remain
 
stable
 
under
 
small
 
perturbations,
 
matter
 
traversal,
 
and
 
semiclassical
 
backreaction.
 
[9]
 
[33]
 
[50]
 
5.
 
Causality
 
and
 
Time-Machine
 
Risk
 
(CTR):
 
Whether
 
the
 
concept
 
allows
 
CTC
 
formation
 
directly
 
or
 
indirectly,
 
and
 
whether
 
chronology
 
protection
 
likely
 
destabilizes
 
it.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
[47]
 
6.
 
Human
 
and
 
Macroscopic
 
Matter
 
Survivability
 
(HMS):
Whether
 
tidal
 
forces,
 
radiation,
 
and
 
acceleration
 
requirements
 
can
 
be
 
kept
 
within
 
tolerable
 
bounds
 
for
 
human-scale
 
objects.
 
[4]
 
[21]

===== PAGE 50 =====
50
 
7.
 
Universality
 
of
 
Destination
 
(UD):
 
Whether
 
the
 
mechanism
 
can
 
realistically
 
support
 
“anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe”
 
transport
 
without
 
requiring
 
pre-installed
 
infrastructure
 
at
 
the
 
destination.
 
[22]
 
[13]
 
A
 
concept
 
is
 
“promising”
 
only
 
if
 
it
 
performs
 
acceptably
 
across
 
all
 
criteria,
 
not
 
merely
 
one.
 
For

may
 
be

require
 
an
 
impossible
 
stress–energy
 
source;
 
conversely,

negative-energy
 
effect
 
may
 
be
 
experimentally
 
real
 
but
 
too
 
small
 
to
 
scale.

13.4
 
The
 
“Minimum
 
Necessary
 
Breakthroughs”
 
for
 
Macroscopic
 
Teleportation
 
The
 
unified
 
analysis
 
suggests
 
that
 
the
 
following
 
breakthroughs
 
are
 
necessary
 
before
 
human-scale
 
teleportation
 
can
 
be
 
contemplated
 
even
 
in
 
principle:
 
●
 
Macroscopic
 
NEC
 
violation
 
with
 
controlled
 
spatial
 
profiles
 
—
 
consistent
 
with
 
quantum
 
inequalities
 
or
 
with
 
new
 
physics
 
that
 
modifies
 
them.
 
[44]
 
●
 
Stable
 
semiclassical
 
solutions
 
with
 
bounded
 
backreaction
 
—
 
meaning
 
self-consistent
 
solutions
 
to
 
semiclassical
 
Einstein
 
equations.
 
[33]
 
[26]
 
●
 
Causality-preserving
 
geometry
 
design
 
—
 
either
 
by
 
avoiding
 
time-machine
 
configurations
 
or
 
by
 
discovering
 
a
 
physical
 
mechanism
 
enforcing
 
chronology
 
protection
 
without
 
destroying
 
the
 
system.
 
[8]
 
[53]
 
●
 
Human-safe
 
curvature
 
and
 
radiation
 
environment
 
—
 
throat/bubble
 
geometry
 
must
 
be
 
gentle
 
enough
 
to
 
avoid
 
lethal
 
tidal
 
stress.
 
[4]
 
These
 
are
 
not
 
merely
 
engineering
 
difficulties;
 
they
 
represent
 
constraints
 
at
 
the
 
boundary
 
of
 
established
 
physics.
 
 
13.5

Candidate

14.
 
Future
 
Research
 
Roadmap,
 
Implementation
 
Architecture
 
A
 
thesis
 
on
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
that
 
aims
 
to
 
move
 
a
 
human
 
body
 
and
 
large
 
matter
 
anywhere
 
in
 
the
 
universe
 
must

with

realistic

of
 
what
 
kinds

scientific

would

to
 
transform

Warp/bubble

regimes

14.1
 
Technology
 
Stack
 
for
 
Universe-Wide
 
Human
 
Teleportation
 
A
 
complete
 
teleportation
 
technology
 
capable
 
of
 
transporting
 
humans
 
and
 
macroscopic
 
matter
 
would
 
likely
 
require
 
multiple
 
interacting
 
subsystems.
 
In
 
a
 
unified
 
architecture,
 
these
 
subsystems
 
may
 
be
 
expressed
 
as:
 
1.
 
Stress–Energy
 
Source
 
Layer
 
:
  
A
 
physical
 
mechanism
 
to
 
generate
 
the
 
required
 
negative
 
energy
 
density
 
and
 
anisotropic
 
pressures,
 
possibly

14.2
 
Near-Term
 
Experimental
 
Milestones
 
(Physics-Driven)
 
Even

14.2.1
 
Precision
 
Measurements
 
of
 
Vacuum
 
Stress–Energy
 
The
 
Casimir
 
effect
 
demonstrates
 
that
 
vacuum
 
energy
 
is
 
physically
 
real
 
and
 
controllable
 
via
 
boundaries.
 
Future
 
milestones
 
include:
 
●
 
More
 
precise
 
measurements
 
of
 
Casimir
 
forces
 
in
 
diverse
 
geometries.
 
●
 
Better
 
modeling
 
of
 
corrections
 
from
 
conductivity,
 
temperature,
 
and
 
surface
 
roughness.
 
●
 
Exploration
 
of
 
materials
 
and
 
metamaterials
 
for
 
altered
 
vacuum
 
mode
 
structure.
 
[38]
 
[39]
 
[55]
 
Such
 
experiments
 
do
 
not
 
produce
 
teleportation,
 
but
 
they
 
are
 
foundational
 
for
 
any
 
future
 
attempt
 
to
 
engineer
 
vacuum
 
stress–energy
 
profiles.

14.3.1
 
Finding

Realizable
 
NEC-Violating
 
Field
 
Theories

needed

an
 
action.

negative

regimes.

gravity

that

requirements.

from
 
instabilities

as

(negative

vacuum
 
decay.

14.4
 
Far-Future
 
Engineering
 
Pathways
 
(Speculative
 
but
 
Structured)
 
Although
 
far-future
 
engineering
 
is
 
speculative,
 
it
 
must
 
remain
 
logically
 
constrained.
 
The
 
plausible
 
pathway
 
for
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
can
 
be
 
expressed
 
as
 
a
 
sequence
 
of
 
“capability
 
thresholds.”
 
Threshold
 
A:
 
High-Fidelity
 
Vacuum
 
Mode
 
Control
 
The
 
ability
 
to
 
shape
 
vacuum
 
fluctuations
 
in
 
complex
 
3D
 
geometries
 
beyond
 
parallel
 
plates.
 
Threshold
 
B:
 
Mesoscopic
 
Negative
 
Energy
 
Shaping
 
The
 
ability
 
to
 
generate
 
negative
 
energy
 
distributions
 
over
 
centimeter
 
or
 
meter
 
scales
 
(still
 
far
 
below
 
wormhole
 
requirements,
 
but
 
a
 
massive
 
leap
 
beyond
 
present
 
technology).
 
Threshold
 
C:
 
Prototype
 
Exotic
 
Geometry
 
in
 
Controlled
 
Environments
 
A
 
stable
 
localized
 
spacetime
 
distortion
 
of
 
measurable
 
magnitude—something
 
that
 
changes
 
geodesic
 
paths
 
or
 
time
 
dilation
 
in
 
a
 
way
 
not
 
attributable
 
to
 
ordinary
 
mass-energy.
 
Threshold
 
D:
 
Traversable
 
Micro-Wormhole
 
Stabilization
 
Stabilizing
 
a
 
microscopic
 
wormhole
 
or
 
nontrivial
 
connectivity
 
region
 
long
 
enough
 
for
 
controlled
 
signal
 
transmission.
 
Threshold
 
E:
 
Human-Safe
 
Macroscopic
 
Geometry
 
Scaling
 
the
 
stable
 
region
 
to
 
human
 
traversal
 
with
 
safe
 
tidal
 
gradients
 
and
 
radiation.
 
Each
 
threshold
 
requires
 
multiple
 
breakthroughs
 
and
 
is
 
not
 
guaranteed
 
by
 
known
 
physics.
 
However,
 
this
 
staged
 
approach
 
allows
 
one
 
to
 
define
 
what
 
“progress”
 
would
 
look
 
like
 
without
 
assuming
 
immediate
 
feasibility.
 
 
15.
 
Limitations
 
of
 
the
 
Present
 
Analysis
 
The
 
present
 
work
 
is
 
a
 
theoretical
 
study
 
conducted
 
within
 
the
 
frameworks
 
of
 
classical
 
general
 
relativity,
 
semiclassical
 
gravity,
 
and
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory
 
in
 
curved
 
spacetime.
 
The
 
discussion
 
relies
 
on
 
idealized
 
metric
 
models
 
(including

and
 
representative
 
warp/bubble
 
geometries)
 
to
 
identify
 
the
 
geometric
 
and
 
stress–energy
 
requirements
 
for
 
macroscopic
 
shortcut
 
transport.
 
Consequently,
 
several
 
results
 
are
 
expressed
 
as
 
scaling
 
relations
 
and
 
constraint-based
 
arguments
 
rather
 
than
 
as
 
fully
 
parameter-fixed
 
numerical
 
predictions.
 
A
 
major

is
 
that
 
the
 
renormalized
 
stress–energy
 
tensor
 
⟨
T̂ ren
  
is
 
not
 
computed
 
explicitly
 
for
 
a

quantum
 
field
 
model
 
in
 
a
 
fully
 
self-consistent
 
background
 
geometry.
 
Instead,
 
negative
 
energy
 
is
 
treated
 
through
 
established
 
examples
 
(such
 
as
 
vacuum
 
boundary
 
effects)
 
and
 
through
 
general
 
quantum
 
inequality
 
bounds,
 
while
 
the
 
dynamical
 
semiclassical
 
backreaction
 
problem—solving
 
the
 
coupled
 
system
 
G
μ
v
[g]
 
=
 
(8π
G/c
⁴
)
 
⟨
 
T̂ ren
  
is
 
not
 
solved
 
numerically.
 
As

result,
 
stability
 
conclusions
 
are
 
necessarily
 
limited
 
to
 
qualitative
 
and
 
analytic
 
insights,
 
rather
 
than
 
time-dependent
 
simulations
 
of
 
realistic
 
perturbations
 
and
 
feedback.

17.
 
Conclusion
 
This
 
thesis
 
has
 
presented
 
a
 
unified
 
theoretical
 
framework
 
for
 
exotic
 
physics
 
teleportation
 
of
 
macroscopic
 
matter,
 
integrating
 
eight
 
major
 
concepts:
 
traversable
 
wormholes,
 
negative
 
energy
 
engineering
 
(Casimir-based),
 
quantum
 
vacuum
 
state
 
manipulation,
 
space-time
 
metric
 
engineering
 
(warp/bubble),
 
topological
 
space-time
 
surgery,
 
closed
 
timelike
 
curves,
 
quantum
 
foam
 
shortcuts,
 
and
 
energy-to-geometry
 
conversion.
 
The
 
central
 
unifying
 
principle
 
is
 
that
 
teleportation
 
is
 
a
 
geometry-engineering
 
problem
 
governed
 
by
 
Einstein’s
 
field
 
equations,
 
requiring
 
stress–energy
 
distributions
 
that
 
violate
 
classical
 
energy
 
conditions.
 
Traversable
 
wormholes
 
remain
 
the
 
most
 
coherent
 
candidate
 
for
 
human
 
and
 
large-matter
 
teleportation
 
because
 
they
 
provide
 
explicit
 
geometric
 
shortcuts
 
and
 
continuous
 
worldlines,
 
but
 
they
 
require
 
NEC
 
violation
 
and
 
face
 
severe
 
stability
 
and
 
causality
 
constraints.
 
Warp/bubble
 
metrics
 
offer
 
destination
 
flexibility
 
but
 
appear
 
even
 
more
 
demanding
 
in
 
negative
 
energy
 
magnitude
 
and
 
stability.
 
Topology
 
surgery
 
and
 
quantum
 
foam
 
belong
 
to
 
quantum
 
gravity
 
regimes,
 
while
 
CTC-assisted
 
transport
 
threatens
 
causal
 
consistency
 
and
 
is
 
likely
 
forbidden
 
or
 
destabilized
 
by
 
chronology
 
protection.
 
[4]
 
[21]
 
[54]
 
[8]
 
[16]
 
The
 
strongest
 
conclusion
 
is
 
that
 
macroscopic
 
teleportation
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
“hard
 
engineering”;
 
it
 
is
 
likely
 
a
 
frontier
 
problem
 
of
 
fundamental
 
physics.
 
If
 
future
 
scientific
 
advances
 
enable
 
controlled
 
negative
 
energy
 
shaping,
 
stable
 
semiclassical
 
exotic
 
geometries,
 
and
 
causality-safe
 
global
 
structures,
 
then
 
teleportation
 
could
 
move
 
from
 
theoretical
 
possibility
 
to
 
engineering
 
concept.
 
Until
 
then,
 
teleportation
 
remains
 
an
 
extraordinary
 
objective
 
that
 
usefully
 
clarifies
 
the
 
boundaries
 
of
 
general
 
relativity,
 
quantum
 
field
 
theory,
 
and
 
quantum
 
gravity.
